Three Prada Obsessives Unpack Miuccia’s Defining Decade

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Bazaar’s Lynette Nylander and Camille Freestone join Laia Garcia-Furtado for a roundtable on her new book, Prada in the Nineties

Prada is as much an adjective as it is a noun. My colleagues and I often describe wardrobe twists, like a bejeweled brooch on a Peter Pan collar or an embroidered sock peeking out of a Mary Jane, as “very Prada.” But what is it about these little notes of character that signals “Prada” in our brain?

That’s one of the questions Bazaar’s deputy culture director, Laia Garcia-Furtado, answers in her new book, Prada in the Nineties. (Published by Batsford, it drops today.) Her deep dive into one of the brand’s most pivotal decades reaches far beyond the heroin-chic label we tend to slap on ’90s fashion. From runway reviews to cultural anecdotes, Garcia-Furtado takes us backstage, into the life and personality of Miuccia Prada, the industry’s north star of intellectual fashion.

Mrs. Prada’s arsenal of off-color prints and skirts have inspired generations of women to aspire to an ugly-chic kind of cool; looking at her today, it’s easy to find ourselves lost in her world. But in harkening back to the nascent stages of her career as a designer, Garcia-Furtado reminds us that the throughline of her genius lies in her sense of self.

It’s a story that began with nylon, an unexpected choice for a member of the Prada family, with its legacy of selling bespoke leather goods to royal clientele. Yet Mrs. Prada’s choice to bring nylon totes and duffels to the luxury market was not merely driven by rebellion—it was a manifestation of her own desire for a well-designed bag that embraced utility. And the groundbreaking success of the bags demonstrated that her dreams were shared by other women—a hint of where Prada would take the brand.

Garcia-Furtado understands this lore firsthand. After all, she’s spent a lot of time in Mrs. Prada’s shoes—well, the ones she designed, at least. She began building her own archive of Miuccia merch in college with a pair of woven leather Mary Janes by Prada’s little sister, Miu Miu. A lucky eBay find from the spring 2001 collection, they were marked by interlaced checks of leather with pops of plum purple and canvas buckle straps. She has continued to build her collection ever since.

Writing the book gave Garcia-Furtado carte blanche to dig into the nitty-gritty details that unite women of varying tastes and lifestyles as proud members of Prada’s hive. Below, she gathers with Bazaar’s fellow Prada obsessees, executive digital director Lynette Nylander and senior fashion writer Camille Freestone, to unpack all the things that have placed Mrs. Prada in a league of her own, from the ’90s to now.

Prada in the Ninteties by Laia Garcia-Furtado

Jennifer Jenkins: What inspired you to go on such a deep dive on Miuccia Prada?

Laia Garcia-Furtado: There are very specific images that we go to when we think of Prada in the ’90s, but all of those collections are from the late ’90s. So I thought, "Wait, what about the early '90s?" When I started looking up those collections, I realized that what I was really doing was tracing how Prada went from almost an independent, fledgling designer to an icon in a period of ten years, which is really crazy. I love to go down a research hole; that's actually my favorite thing. It was just great to have an excuse.

Lynette Nylander: One of the things that struck me about going through Laia’s book, and my own personal understanding of Prada in the '90s, is that it was really foundational. A lot of the signatures and markers of what Prada is today were made in that formative time. One of my favorite things about being part of the Prada hive—and I think we all identify as that—is that she has carefully constructed a brand where you can mine from its past. She's very okay with the idea that something is a rehashed idea from a '90s collection in the 2000s, in the 2010s, in the 2020s. Everything is meant to be in dialogue with everything else. That's really refreshing in a world where designers are making very distinct collections and basically almost asking you to do away with whatever you bought last year and buy into what's “now.” She very much respects the past, which is why I think a book on the '90s is brilliant.

Camille Freestone: Also, the designs are so pared back. We think now of much more heavy-handed embellishment when we imagine Prada. Part of it was that it was the '90s and it was just a simpler time. Runways have just gotten more megawatt altogether. But the Prada collections are quite pure. The design is quite simple in the best way, so they let her creative touches shine in such a way that feels very present.

LGF: I mean everything old is new, et cetera. Going back to what you were saying about the simple shapes, even when things are heavily embellished and there's a lot of beading and sequins, it never feels like putting on a bunch of stuff for the sake of putting on a bunch of stuff. Even if it's super over-the-top, you think, “It's exactly what it needs to be.” It feels very real. One of my favorite things I read while I was doing research is a quote where she's saying how in movies and in art, you're allowed to bring in real life and it's allowed to be ugly and regular, but fashion was a place where you weren't allowed to do that, and she wanted to change that. When I read that, everything from those old collections made so much sense.

But even now, I think you can read a collection, even if she and Raf are saying that it's about one thing, and you look at it, you're like, "It's really about something else."

LN: Yeah, and that's always been a part of that duality. And I mean, even in the inception of Miu Miu, she talks about it as this other side of her personality. She's someone who is really in dialogue with her more fantastical, imagined self. And I think what was interesting about going through the '90s collections was that it was fantastical, but again, more real.

But I want to talk about what you learned. They always say hindsight is 20/20, but I don't think we were lucky enough to understand what a unique time the ‘90s were. It was pre-social-media. Celebrity culture had hit this apex, and Miuccia was there as this brand that carried and collected a lot of celebrity in a way that is not how it is now.

LGF: It wasn’t grody! And I think that was especially evident when she launched menswear, and John Malkovich was her model or Willem Dafoe—I mean, now we all love Willem Dafoe, of course, but it was very different from what everyone else was doing.

And another thing that's really interesting is that we have a vision of the ’90s, but the first couple of years of the ‘90s are still the ’80s. It takes years for the prevailing aesthetic that we all remember to rise up. Going back, Prada was a heritage label, but she launched ready-to-wear in the late ‘80s. And it wasn't like when Vuitton launched ready-to-wear, when everybody understood it as a major brand. Prada was by no means in its flop era, but it wasn’t as well known as Vuitton. It was not the same.

So when she started, critics were writing things like “Prada also showed a collection.” There wasn't even a long review. And then she sort of started getting talked about in the same breath as Martin Margiela or Ann Demeulemeester, like, “These people are the plucky new designers.” We think she's so untouchable now and it's like she almost can do no wrong, but you can go back and see how it all started and see her be like, "I hated this collection actually, and I'll never do that again." I mean, my kingdom for any designer that would say that now—to just be that honest.

JJ: You touched a lot on her unapologetic way of expressing herself, even while taking in different cultural references and touchpoints. Was there anything in particular you were most surprised to learn about her?

LGF: My favorite trivia is that she got married on Valentine's Day.

LGF: She's a romantic. I feel like that says so much.

CF: It's amazing. She was in the Communist Party of Italy, and so not of the fashion world at all, but so endlessly fascinated by it.

LGF: It's so funny because it's very much like her parents said, "Okay, you've had your fun. Now, come back home, join the family business.” And she wanted to be an actor—the mime thing! In one interview, she said, "My favorite part was when we had to do really specific body control stuff." And you can totally see her just being so good at that.

LN: Well, I think that she's so good at it because I can't think of a designer, at least in more modern history, that really sort of mines from herself. Have you ever noticed, if you read a part of a review, or you’ve seen those videos of her backstage, it's never like, "I was looking at the art of Georgia O’Keeffe”?

LN: She doesn't even mine it from a place. She isn't even saying, "Oh, I was walking down the piazza in Milan." It's not about that—it's this higher sense of self. I think about the fall/winter 2026 collection, with Bella Hadid and the four rounds of looks. And of course she did that because she's the only person who would do that, because those are the things that ticktock around in the caverns of her mind when we're all fast asleep.

CF: Well, she's oddly practical. She's kind of utilitarian. And I think now I'm just saying a different thing in different words, but you think of a crystal embellishment when you think of Prada, you think of a quirk, but it's all rooted in a really utilitarian approach. I feel like this is clothing, not art.

LGF: It's also because that's how she dresses. I mean, yes, are her high heels too high for us to walk and not get blisters? Yes, but we still wear them. It's fine. But she designs clothes that she wants to wear, and we always look at what she's wearing at the end of the show to see how she's styling.

LN: She's the model that we're looking for at the end of the show.

CF: Then it's funny to put her back in dialogue with a Martin Margiela; they both were subversive in their own ways, but hers was lensed through an idea that these are just clothes. You need to get dressed in them!

LGF: There is something about the two of them. Margiela never wanted to be interviewed, but when you look back at a lot of the profiles of her, there will be editorials where it's designers with their muses, and she's almost always by herself. She's her own muse.

It's just her. It's like her looking in a mirror. It's her and her son, or it's her by herself, but it's such a clear vision. And I mean, I think it also makes a difference because a lot of the male designers need a muse…but for her, it's always her.

CF: I'm also always fascinated by the “designer uniform.” There are so many examples. Phoebe Philo, Michael Kors, Jonathan Anderson: everyone takes their bow, and it's almost like they're the antithesis of their clothes. So obviously, we are always talking about a woman designing for women, but in that particular context of those big houses, she's one of the few who is just dressing just like her collection.

LGF: And it's amazing the few times she's come out wearing pants.

LGF: It's so rare! You're like, "Oh my god, the slim cigarette.”

LN: I have two things I want to say to the idea that she doesn't really have a muse. The muse is herself, but somehow that doesn't come across as arrogant. It's not the opposite. It comes across as, “I just do what I know I’ll respond to and hopefully other people like it.” And I think there's something really earnest about it.

CF: There's also no ideal of the perfect woman, either.

LN: Yes, in fact, I don't think she even really deals or trades in ideals.

I also want to talk about the colors. I mean, if I think about Prada, I just think about this kaleidoscopic universe of color. She can go anywhere. She can put any color combination together. It's kind of genius. But particularly in the '90s… they're all quite muted. You have this line from Robin Givhan in the book, “The greens hovered somewhere between shades of slime and mold. The browns were”—I mean, this is the beauty of the Robin Givhan written line—“the browns were murky, the color of water as it stagnates over a long, steamy summer. The dizzy and crisscross patterns were reminiscent of UHF static or an old-fashioned labyrinth in rusted plumbing.”

LGF: It's funny though because Miuccia Prada hated all of those collections.

LN: I think if there was ever going to be a brand that's going to respect the fact that Robin Givhan ripped her to shreds, it's going to be her. I think she believes in the idea of free thought, and that was Givhan’s thought and put so beautifully.

But I do want to talk about the colors, because I just think it's so specific of how I think about Prada, but also how I think about my own relationship to color. I don't know if you feel like that, but probably in an inadvertent way, she has given me the confidence and the permission to put together these really disparate color combinations.

LGF: I mean, I think that collection that Robin is talking about, from spring ’96 (which is the root cause of my everything; I had a slime green room—I love slime green) is really interesting. Even before she got into the cool color combinations that we know her from, those early '90s collections, even when it was like, “Here's a bunch of beautiful white dresses,” suddenly there would be weird goldenrod silk and a weird orange. The color thing was always there from the beginning. And when you look at the collections throughout the years, you see how she gets a little bit more confident in that and starts mixing it. But that punch of weirdness was present from the beginning.

CF: But somehow, I think, "Why do I like this slime shade? Why do I like this brown? Why do I like this purple?" But all of a sudden, she's made me want purple.

LN: I hate purple in any other context than hers—I need purple.

CF: All of a sudden I want purple. And there's these weird plaids that all of a sudden make me think, "Oh, I need that."

LGF: Also, in the late '90s, she does collections with things embellished with mirrors, and she was like, "Well, everyone is being a hippie and doing this, so I also did it,” but she did it extra crazy. And I love when she is engaging.

Going back to what you said about her being probably one of the few designers that is like, "This review panned me, it's fine,” I feel like she really was engaging with what was being written about her.

One season editors were like, "There are no suits. This is outrageous! Where are all the suits?" So then the next season she opened the collection with a suit and it's very like, "You're welcome. Are you happy now?” I like to see her engage, not that she doesn't do that anymore, but I think in the ’90s, it was a little bit more obvious.

LN: And now she kind of hovers above it all. I want to talk about 1998. Her ascent was complete, you say, in 1998, when she was now at over a half a billion dollars in sales. She landed at 15 in Vogue’s list of the most influential brands. Other women at the top 20 were Donna Karan, Angela Missoni, and Mariuccia Mandelli, and Miuccia is one of the only working designers left. What was happening in 1998, and what collection is that?

LGF: It's the one with the cool red-and-white boots that are space-age. It's all very space-age mod. ‘98 is the time when she went all-in on the millennial aesthetic that everyone was into, when she did her sports thing.

CF: I also think she doesn't deal very much in volume. That's not really her game.

LN: Thank god. I don't know if I'd want a poofy Prada.

LGF: Yeah. But it's so crazy—in a decade you go from reviews that say, "Oh, Prada also showed a collection," to, "Everyone is focused on whatever she does."

LGF: I think she just became more confident in her vision, which was different from everyone else. And I mean, Tom Ford was also doing something at Gucci; there was that vibe shift, and she was part of it, but it was different. From the beginning, she was always the thinking woman's sex-appeal designer, and that just stayed. She was very in tune with the culture, but she wasn't just doing reproductions of what you could see. She was injecting it with her own aesthetic.

You can look at something and say, "Oh, this was her way of doing grunge," but it was not grunge. It was her own thing.

LN: What was that thing? I mean, do you look at broader context and get a sense of the music she listens to or the boys she kissed or the movies she liked?

LGF: Yeah, I think it's very much a movies thing. I feel like you get a sense of her upbringing. The Mary Jane—the shoes she wore as a little girl—were always a thing. She got married in a dress and a coat made by a childrenswear designer. The little socks—you start seeing those reflections of her life, and they're more apparent the more that you know her, but I don't think it's the kind of thing where you need to know what it is in order to like it. And I think there is something about it where it's for grown women. Young people also like it, but her clothes are not clothes for young teen girls.

LN: Was that also something you learned from your research?

LGF: I mean, technically, I think Miu Miu was supposed to be the younger brand, and the vibe did feel a little bit younger, but Prada always felt a little bit adult. Even when you look at the suits or the proper little silk dresses, you're like, "These are clothes for adults."

CF: I feel like that's also where the styling sneaks in. You’ll see the childlike Mary Jane with the suit and maybe one little brooch or something that is so subtle…but it's not hitting you over the head.

LGF: Yeah, because I think there's also a thing where you always look up to something. You know how you read Seventeen magazine when you're 12? And I think there is something about Prada where no matter how old you are, it still feels just beyond where you currently are.

LN: Every time I buy a piece, it still feels, in a weird way, unattainable, or I question if I'm mature or smart or cool enough.

LGF: And then you look in the mirror, and it feels like it feels exactly where you should be.

CF: It is like, "Am I a woman? Am I a glamorous woman?"

LN: Yeah. Maybe it's just because we think about her so much as the likeness of that.

CF: Well, we have made her the ideal almost. She is the one to be judged against, for so many people, but then that makes it hard to articulate what she actually is.

LGF: But even her glamour—there is something attainable about it. She doesn't wear crazy makeup, and she doesn't style her hair in any kind of way. It's just her holding her little scarf, and you think, "I need a scarf to hold!" Going back to her interest in real life, it's just so her. And who doesn't want to just be like themselves? You want to be her, but you really just want to be you.

Kemper (KMPR) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Rate actions lifted margins as management traded policy volume for profitability.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Kemper Corporation (KMPR +3.74%) reported improved sequential underwriting results as rate increases in California and other key markets began earning into the portfolio. Management stated the company is prioritizing profitability and margin restoration over policy growth, leading to an expected decline in policies in force. The company consolidated its distribution and marketing capabilities under a unified leadership team to coordinate lead generation and digital sales across product lines. Management reported that geographic diversification is a priority, with Florida and Texas representing a growing share of the personal auto book to reduce concentration in California.

Operator: Good morning, ladies and gentlemen, and welcome to Kemper's Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Conference Call. My name is Jen, and I will be your coordinator today. [Operator Instructions] As a reminder, this conference call is being recorded for replay purposes. I would now like to introduce your host for today's conference call, Michael Marinaccio, Kemper's Vice President of Corporate Development and Investor Relations. Mr. Marinaccio, you may begin.

Michael Marinaccio: Thank you. Good morning, everyone, and welcome to Kemper's discussion of our third quarter 2026 results. This morning, you'll hear from Steve McAnena, Kemper's President and CEO; and Brad Camden, Kemper's Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer. We'll make a few opening remarks to provide context around our third quarter results, followed by a Q&A session. During the interactive portion of the call, our presenters will be joined by John Boschelli, Kemper's Executive Vice President and Chief Investment Officer; Chris Flint, Kemper's Executive Vice President and Chief Distribution and Marketing Officer; Eric Kappler, Kemper's Executive Vice President and Head of P&C; and Jennifer Kopps-Wagner, Kemper's Executive Vice President and Head of Life.

After the markets closed yesterday, we issued our earnings release, filed our Form 10-Q with the SEC and published our earnings presentation and financial supplement. You can find these documents in the Investors section of our website, kemper.com. Our discussion today may contain forward-looking statements within the meaning of the safe harbor provisions of the Private Securities Litigation Reform Act of 1995. These statements include, but are not limited to, the company's outlook on its future results of operation and financial condition. Our actual future results and financial condition may differ materially from these statements. For information on additional risks that may impact these forward-looking statements, please refer to our 2025 Form 10-K and our third quarter earnings release.

This morning's discussion also includes non-GAAP financial measures we believe are meaningful to investors. In our financial supplement, earnings presentation and earnings release, we have defined and reconciled all the non-GAAP financial measures to GAAP where required in accordance with SEC rules. You can find each of these documents in the Investors section of our website, kemper.com. All comparative references will be to the corresponding 2025 period unless otherwise stated. I'll now turn the call over to Steve.

Stephen McAnena: Thanks, Michael. Good morning, everyone, and thank you for joining us. As I noted in our second quarter earnings call, we're taking actions to strengthen execution and accountability. Today, I can report that those actions are taking hold. The progress we made in the second quarter continued and our operating results improved. Two disciplines are behind these results: relentlessly reviewing our data and acting with urgency. Over the past 5 months, I spent much of my time with our employees, agents and business partners. They told me what makes Kemper special: talented people, deep relationships and a drive to serve customers the broader market often overlooks. They were just as clear about what needs to change, and we listened.

Their input, along with my own assessment, informs 3 immediate priorities: restoring profitability, positioning Kemper for durable growth and strengthening our leadership team. Together, they give us a clear path forward, a path I believe in. We'll take them one at a time, beginning with profitability because it's foundational to everything we do. Let's start with personal auto. Our rate and non-rate actions have improved the underlying combined ratio by nearly 8 points since the first quarter. That's real progress. And while we're pleased with these gains, we're not yet earning an underwriting profit, and we won't be satisfied until we do. California, our largest market, is a big part of that story.

Those same steps reduced California's underlying combined ratio by more than 7 points since the first quarter. But all of this came with a trade-off, and I want to be direct about it. We acted decisively, and we expected premium and policy counts to decline and they did. It wasn't an easy call, but it was the right one. And as margins improve, our focus turns to stabilizing production and returning to growth at a pace our profitability justifies. Turning to commercial auto. Last quarter, we said we'd put margins ahead of volume, take more rate and tighten our underwriting. Since then, margins have narrowed, underscoring the need for those actions and new business growth has slowed as we expected.

We'll stay the course and accept modest growth as we work back toward our targets. Beyond auto, Kemper Life continues to deliver dependable earnings, steady cash flow and valuable diversification. Together with our high-quality investment portfolio, it gives us a solid foundation while we improve P&C results. Restoring profitability is where we start, not where we stop. With every point of margin, we earn more of the right to grow. And that brings me to our second priority, positioning Kemper for durable growth, a kind that's profitable and built to last. We're pursuing that growth through our newly formed distribution marketing organization led by Chris Flint.

We created this structure in September with a simple objective, reach more customers more productively across multiple channels at a lower cost. Many consumers value personal guidance and service, and we intend to deepen those relationships, not replace them. At the same time, people want flexibility in how they engage. Our job is to make connecting with us seamless wherever and whenever they choose. Doing that effectively requires one team that coordinates tactics across every channel. Over the next several months, we'll run structured tests across lead generation, lead management and lead fulfillment to learn what drives results. We'll judge every tactic on performance, we'll scale what works and we'll stop what doesn't.

In time, this will become the groundwork for future growth. Strategy sets direction, but people and culture deliver the results, which brings me to our third priority, leadership. We need leaders who are highly accountable, make timely decisions and work as one company, not in silos. That's the leadership team and culture we're building at Kemper. We've added experience and depth to the team across P&C, life, claims and distribution. We're also simplifying how decisions get made and how work gets done. You won't find those changes on an income statement, but you'll see them in how quickly we move and how consistently we execute. Before I close, I want to recognize our commitment to the communities we serve.

Through the Kemper Foundation, our philanthropic arm, we sponsor Read Conmigo, a program that puts resources directly into the hands of teachers and schools to enable dual language education. It helps children build the skills and confidence they need to succeed. We bring that same spirit to our customers every day, helping people protect what matters and supporting them through some of life's toughest moments. So let me close where I began. Our actions are taking hold. We're restoring profitability with discipline, building the capabilities for durable growth and leading with the team that owns the outcome. We're encouraged by our progress, but we're not declaring victory.

There's much work ahead, and I have every confidence in this organization as we move Kemper forward. Thank you. With that, I'll turn the call over to Brad for a closer look at our financial results.

Bradley Camden: Thank you, and good morning, everyone. As Steve shared, we had a strong quarter, but we have more work to do. Net income was $52.4 million or $0.88 per diluted share and adjusted consolidated net operating income was $59.9 million or $1.01 per diluted share. Our financial results reflect continued progress in underwriting performance and consistent contributions from Life and investments. I'll begin my comments with our operating businesses, followed by expense efficiency, capital and investments. Specialty P&C reported a combined ratio of 99.2%, down 4.8 points sequentially. Reported results benefited from $23 million of favorable prior year reserve development. The underlying combined ratio also improved to 101.7% from 102.3%.

The improvement in Specialty P&C was driven largely by personal auto, where we're beginning to see the cumulative benefits of our rate and non-rate actions. The reported combined ratio improved to 99.3% compared with 105% last quarter, while the underlying combined ratio improved another 2.2 points to 103.3%. The biggest improvement in the book has been in our largest state, California. Its underlying combined ratio improved 5.2 points sequentially to 102.1%. Since the beginning of 2025, we've received approval for cumulative rate increases of approximately 13% in California personal auto, including approximately 50% in the bodily injury coverage. Bodily injury has been our greatest source of margin pressure following last year's increase in the financial responsibility limits.

These rate increases, together with our rate and non-rate actions, are contributing to improved results. We expect additional benefits as the more recent increases earn into the book. As Steve mentioned, our actions to restore profitability came with an expected trade-off, lower production. Personal auto earned premiums were $600 million, down 7% sequentially, while policies in force declined approximately 10% during the quarter. While restoring profitability remains our top priority, geographic diversification is also important to the long-term performance of personal auto. We believe a more balanced portfolio that reduces concentration risk will support more consistent underwriting results and profitable growth over time.

As we expand in other markets, we expect California to represent a smaller percentage of our personal auto portfolio over time. We're making progress toward that objective. California now represents 63% of policies compared with 67% earlier this year. Florida and Texas, our second and third largest markets, now account for 28% of personal auto policies, an increase of 5.5 percentage points since the first quarter. Together, they represent approximately 79% of new business direct written premium. Moving to commercial auto. Our decision to moderate production is reflected in slower policy growth this quarter. Policies in force increased 0.7%, while earned premiums increased 3.6% sequentially to $258 million.

The business continues to generate an underwriting profit on an underlying basis, although margins have narrowed. The reported combined ratio was 99% compared with 101.2% last quarter. The underlying combined ratio increased 4.1 points sequentially to 97.8%, reflecting elevated loss costs, primarily in bodily injury. We've taken additional pricing and underwriting actions to address these trends. We'll continue to moderate PIF growth until we're confident that earned pricing is outpacing loss trends and that margins are on a sustainable path toward our long-term targets. Turning to Life. We continue to see consistent earnings from this business. Adjusted net operating income was $20 million this quarter compared with $18 million last quarter and $19 million a year ago.

Normalized revenues increased modestly to $153 million compared with $152 million last quarter and $148 million a year ago. Average annual premium issued per policy increased more than 6% year-over-year, reflecting our disciplined approach to pricing and underwriting. Beyond the performance of our operating businesses, expense management remains an important priority. Since launching our restructuring effort in 2025, we have implemented actions generating approximately $90 million in annualized run rate savings. These efforts have helped us maintain a healthy expense ratio despite declining premium volumes. We continue to identify opportunities to simplify operations and improve efficiency. As production stabilizes and returns to growth, we expect to benefit from greater scale and continued expense discipline, supporting stronger operating leverage.

Moving to our balance sheet. Our capital and liquidity position remains strong. Parent company liquidity was $727 million at quarter end, including $91 million of cash and investments and $636 million of available borrowing capacity. Debt to capital was 28%, essentially unchanged from last quarter. Our insurance subsidiaries remain well capitalized with estimated risk-based capital ratios of 625% for life and 230% for P&C. Together, holding company liquidity and subsidiary capital provide financial flexibility to support our businesses as we execute our profitability initiatives. Turning to our investment portfolio. It remains an important source of earnings, supported by its size, diversification and credit quality.

The portfolio totaled approximately $8.3 billion, including $6.3 billion in fixed maturities, of which 69% were rated A or higher. Net investment income was $107 million compared with $105 million in the second quarter. Pretax equivalent annualized book yield on our core portfolio was 4.8% compared with average investment-grade new money yields of approximately 5.9%. With new money yields above our existing portfolio yield, we see an opportunity to boost investment income as cash flows are reinvested depending on portfolio turnover and market conditions. Overall, we're encouraged by the financial progress this quarter. We're focused on further improving underwriting profitability, building capabilities for durable growth and maintaining financial strength. With that, operator, we'd be happy to take questions.

Operator: [Operator Instructions] Your first question comes from the line of Gregory Peters with Raymond James.

Charles Peters: So I'll focus for the first question on the California personal auto business. And I guess it's one question in 2 parts. But given the decline in PIF that corresponds in the improvement in profitability, just wondering where you think the PIF numbers might settle out over the next year or 2? And maybe you could just throw in the commentary on the insurance -- the new insurance commissioner and any perspectives you have on the ability to get further rate increases in California.

Stephen McAnena: Greg, this is Steve. Thanks for the question. I'll take the sort of the first one, the second part first, the commissioner, and then I'll respond to your question about PIF and give you a little context as to how we're thinking about California and maybe ask Brad and Eric to chime in afterwards with any color they may want to add. So if I pull back, the reality is every couple of years, there are new Department of Insurance administrations. From our perspective, if you go back in time and look at where we are currently, we've had pretty strong relations with our departments of insurance, and we work really hard at that.

And we would expect that to continue. And that's both at the top and throughout the organization. Second, with any new administration oftentimes comes change. And I'd say, from our perspective, we have good teams in place to sort of look at what's on the docket, what it means for us and then build strategies to both comply with any changes, but also navigate in that new environment. So we feel pretty good about how we navigate any changes in the insurance department and frankly, look forward to in California, working with Commissioner Kim.

So California, I'm going to give you a little context as to how we're thinking about things and then hand it over, like I said, to Brad and Eric. If you just sort of pull back and look at the state, I think at our peak in Q1, our combined ratio underlying was around 109. We're down to 102. That's not by accident. That's very deliberate as a result of the actions we took, rate and non-rate. And we feel pretty good about that improvement. Our expectation is, and the team will talk about this in a little more depth, that we'll continue to earn in the benefits associated from all the rate and non-rate actions into '27.

At the same time, as you pointed out, it was intentional. We have allowed PIF to decline. Frankly, if you ask why what happened, the punchline there is we got ahead of the market. So we saw what happened in 2025. We took action, and we continue to sort of punch away at rate that we need. You're starting to see other carriers do the same. So if you look at the CDI website, you'll see a bunch of filings with quite significant increases. And so we feel like we're doing the right things. We're okay with the trade-off that we made. I can't give you an exact quarter when profit -- because when profit will be restored.

We feel good about the momentum and the trajectory we're on into 2027. Our plans and our view is to make sure we have good line of sight to profitability. And as we start to see that and feel comfortable, we put together a couple of good quarters, we will then start marching towards PIF stabilization, which oftentimes lags profit restoration by a couple of quarters. And so you can kind of think about 2027, making some progress to sort of drop below 100 and then a couple of quarters after that, PIF stabilization. So hopefully, that gives you a flavor for how we thought about it.

I'll hand it to Eric to maybe provide a little more color on some of the things we're doing in California and add any comments that I made.

Eric Kappler: Yes. Thanks, Steve. And Greg, I'll just kind of reinforce what Steve said. So we do think we recognize the loss trends earlier than the market generally and reacted quickly. To Brad's prepared comments, we've had 4 filings approved to date. We think we're very close on getting a fifth filing approved. The competitors are behind us. At most, they've gotten one approved. Some of them have gotten none approved. So we think the market will catch up with us over the next year or so as those filings get approved.

Bradley Camden: Sorry, Greg, I was going to add to Eric's comments. As Steve was mentioning about PIF growth, you asked the question on, hey, when is that going to stabilize? Steve indicated, it's dependent on a lot of different factors. I would just highlight from Eric's comments, as the market takes rate and it gets approved, our rates will become more competitive and our growth will begin to stabilize. But first and foremost, as Steve indicated, we're focused on restoring profitability. Once that's restored, PIF will stabilize and then we will eventually grow. So time dependent, but we're taking all the right actions, and we like what we're seeing.

Charles Peters: My follow-up question, I know you referenced bringing Chris on board to help with distribution and marketing and if I look at your presentation slides, you did spend one of the slides focusing on distribution. Can you -- in the last several months, there's been the emergence of more AI-related distribution alternatives. News came out, as you know. I'm just curious, when I look at the slide on distribution, how you're thinking about it looking forward, especially in an environment where AI becomes more prevalent.

Stephen McAnena: Great question. And I'll -- this is Steve again. I'll start off and hand it over to Chris. And I think it's important to give you a little bit of context as to how I've been thinking, and I think the team would agree with me. As we continue to restore profitability, we looked ahead and said, I think a good analogy is the car and the driveway in February in Chicago. The engine is cold, you want to warm it up. We really want to do things now on the distribution side to position us to be able to grow the right way on the other side of this profit speed bump.

I'd say contextually important to understand where we're coming from before getting into an agenda. We're primarily an agent-based organization, and that's great. The challenge with that is consumers, as you know, want to interact with you whenever, wherever they want. So from my perspective, as I look at the organization, we have very good people, but I would characterize the resources as being somewhat fragmented across different teams. So we really wanted to bring them together under one unified team. Chris' goals are pretty simple. And I kind of bucket them into 3 areas. One is taking our core capabilities and making them bigger, better, stronger.

In plain English, looking at our agency channel and saying, what are the things we can do to have more agents and make them more productive. So really investing in tools, incentives and reevaluating our engagement model with them. So that's one important thing. And I think in the near term, as we sort of move forward, that pillar will have the biggest benefit. There's a second pillar, which is what are some capabilities that we need to build that we absolutely know we need to build. And for me, there's a couple. We need to build lead generation capabilities, so capabilities to drive people to Kemper. And then we need to have the channels to receive them.

So think of digital and inside sales, and we have an opportunity in those 2 areas. And so Chris is really focused primarily on making our existing channels better and then building new capabilities. There's a third pillar, which is -- gets to your question on AI -- and that's really taking new ideas and doing what I would call constructive tests and learns. I think every company is in a different -- at a different level of maturity in terms of their distribution capabilities and AI experiences, number one. Number two, everyone has different priorities.

Our priority right now is focusing on profitability, making sure that Chris has all the resources he needs to deliver what he needs to strengthen and build. And then we will march towards some tests on the agentic side. We do have plans to do some very simple tests to get some learnings, but I don't want to sort of undersell sort of the challenge associated with that, number one. Number two, we need to make sure that we don't let that distract us from delivering capabilities we need to strengthen and build. Chris, if there's anything you'd like to add in terms of what I said, please feel free to jump in.

Christopher Flint: Sure. I think Steve did a nice job sort of covering things. I just want to reinforce that anything that we do, our growth is really gated by 2 things: disciplined and profitable growth and making sure that we show up wherever and whenever our customers want. And if I just think about where we are, as Steve mentioned, really strengthening and building core capabilities of the organization. And for me, that means a few things, really bringing together common capabilities across the organization. So think marketing analytics, lead generation, sales enablement while also maintaining sort of the unique distinct distribution capabilities we have by product line.

Specifically, when I think about our agentic in general from a distribution and marketing perspective. For us, for me, the team, the bigger opportunity is really how AI is changing the way customers discover, evaluate and purchase insurance. And as Steve mentioned, we're going to continue to test and learn in small cycles so that we can be sure that at the right time, we're positioned to compete in an evolving environment.

NH Investors (NHI) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Senior housing now drives 18% of income, but summer occupancy fell short of plans.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

National Health Investors, Inc. (NHI -4.83%) reported a third quarter defined by the completion of its $541.6 million skilled nursing portfolio sale and the subsequent rotation of capital into the senior housing operating segment. Management indicated that the Senior Housing Operating Portfolio now contributes 18% of total net operating income, doubling its share since the beginning of the year. While the same-store portfolio met growth objectives, recently acquired properties experienced operational delays and occupancy growth that trailed management's initial expectations. The company maintained a leverage ratio below its target range while executing $396.2 million in year-to-date investments, positioning the firm for its second largest investment year in company history.

Operator: Good day, everyone, and welcome to the National Health Investors Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Webcast and Conference Call. [Operator Instructions]. It is now my pleasure to hand the floor over to your host, Dana Hambly, Senior Vice President of Finance. Sir, the floor is yours.

Dana Hambly: Thank you, and welcome to the National Health Investors Third Quarter 2026 Conference Call. On the call today are Eric Mendelsohn, President and CEO; Kevin Pascoe, Chief Investment Officer; Todd Siefert, Chief Financial Officer; Chris Maingot, Chief Operating Officer; and David Travis, Chief Accounting Officer. Yesterday, NHI released its third quarter results and conference call information for a press release after market close. Today's remarks may include forward-looking statements, which are subject to risks or uncertainties and are not guarantees of future performance.

Investors are urged to carefully review NHI's filings with the Securities and Exchange Commission, including its Form 10-K for the year ended December 31, 2025, and Form 10-Q for the quarter ended September 30, 2026, for a discussion of these risks. Copies of these filings are available on the SEC's website at sec.gov or on NHI's website at nhireit.com. In addition, today's call may include certain non-GAAP financial measures, reconciliations of which are provided in NHI's earnings release, which has been furnished to the SEC on Form 8-K. Listeners are encouraged to review those reconciliations provided in the earnings release together with all other information provided in that release. I'll now turn the call over to our CEO, Eric Mendelsohn.

D. Mendelsohn: Good morning, and thank you for joining us. This has been a year of significant transition for NHI. We completed the sale of the NHC portfolio on July 1, substantially reduced our skilled nursing exposure and accelerated our investment in private pay senior housing. Our conviction and SHOP remains strong. We continue to see the most attractive risk-adjusted opportunities for our capital in private pay senior housing, where we believe that the industry backdrop provides powerful and sustained tailwinds for our company. Capturing that opportunity requires both disciplined underwriting and consistent execution. While NHI is focused on the long-term opportunity, we understand the importance of delivering consistent quarterly results.

And in that regard, our third quarter SHOP performance fell short of our expectations. Our 15 same-store properties delivered approximately 19.5% year-over-year NOI growth, consistent with our outlook, but recent performance in the non-same-store communities reduced the contribution we expected from the total portfolio in the quarter. We're not satisfied with those results and are addressing the operating performance with our partners. Our longer-term growth outlook for those properties is unchanged. Chris Maingot, our new COO, will discuss the drivers and our response in more detail. The NHC sale provided $555 million in net proceeds to support NHI's repositioning. We expect to recycle more than 95% of those proceeds before year-end, primarily into SHOP and expected SHOP transition investments.

We've already completed more than $395 million in investments year-to-date and expect significant additional closings during the fourth quarter. Based on that activity, we expect 2026 to be our second largest investment year in the company's history. This portfolio repositioning enhances our growth profile and the portfolio's valuation potential as we redeploy capital from skilled nursing assets into senior housing. By increasing our exposure to SHOP, we participate directly in operating upside and strengthen NHI's long-term growth profile. Including our October acquisition, SHOP now contributes approximately 18% of total NOI compared with 9% entering the year, and we expect that share to grow rapidly as our investment focus is primarily towards SHOP opportunities.

We are executing this strategy from a strong financial position. Our leverage is less than 3x, which is comfortably below the low end of our target range, and we have ample capital available to fund our current pipeline. That flexibility allows us to remain selective to pursue investments that meet our return standards, including low to mid-teens unlevered IRRs. Kevin will provide more details on our investment activity, and Todd will review our financial position and full year guidance. We have also strengthened our executive management team and clarified responsibilities to support a larger operating portfolio.

Chris is leading our asset management team and the build-out of our operating platform, while Kevin is focused entirely on business development and expanding our investment relationship. Together with Todd's financial leadership, this structure gives us greater capacity to grow the portfolio and manage it effectively. We're encouraged by the direction of these changes and our objective remains high single-digit to low double-digit organic SHOP NOI growth. Our priorities are clear: improve the operating performance, deploy capital thoughtfully and build the capabilities to support sustainable growth. We believe the strategy and team are in place, and we recognize that operating results will be the measure of our success.

We are focused on delivering to meet the expectations that we establish and earning your confidence through consistent execution. We have made considerable progress during this transitional year, and I have confidence in the company's strategic direction. I believe NHI is exceptionally well positioned to capitalize on the most attractive senior housing environment that I have experienced in my career. I'm excited about the opportunities ahead. With that, I'll turn the call over to Kevin. Kevin?

Kevin Pascoe: Thank you, Eric, and good morning. I'll begin with our recent investment activity and pipeline, then discuss our business development priorities and the broader investment environment. Year-to-date, we have completed $396.2 million in investments at an average yield of 7.5%. That includes $320 million of SHOP investments at an average year 1 yield of 7.3% after routine CapEx. Our SHOP acquisitions have averaged approximately $223,000 per unit, well below estimated replacement costs, providing an attractive basis for operating growth. We recently completed $51.4 million in investments on 2 properties we previously held $42.3 million in construction loans. Our incremental capital investment was a modest $9 million net of the proceeds of these loan payoffs.

These transactions demonstrate our loan-to-own strategy, allowing us to not only establish strategic relationships during development, but ultimately acquire the completed and stabilized properties. Turning to the pipeline. We currently have $285 million under signed letters of intent, including approximately $190 million that we expect to close during the fourth quarter. Those anticipated closings would bring our total 2026 investment to approximately $590 million. The properties we expect to acquire before year-end will initially be structured under net leases with anticipated conversions to SHOP in 2027. This structure allows us to complete the acquisitions in 2026 within the time frame to our planned Section 1031 exchanges with a clear path for future participation in the operating performance going forward.

Beyond the signed LOIs, we are evaluating approximately $400 million comprised primarily in SHOP. We continue to see active flow of investments and enter 2027 with a substantial pipeline. Our focus is on communities in primary and secondary markets with identifiable opportunities to increase NOI through incremental occupancy gains, pricing growth, expense management and targeted capital investment. We continue to expect high single-digit to low double-digit organic NOI growth, supported by specific operating goals with our operating partners at each property. Existing relationships remain an important source of investment activity. All investments completed year-to-date have been relationship-driven. As our SHOP portfolio expands, we intend to build meaningful scale with select group of operators.

Adding communities to established relationships allows us to leverage prior diligence, supports more efficient asset management and deepens alignment with our operators. Recent interest rate movements have prompted additional discussion and negotiations, although we have not yet seen meaningful change in pricing. If higher financing costs persist, this could moderate competition for acquisitions and make new development more difficult to justify. We believe our access to capital, ability to execute and strong operator relationships position us well as a reliable partner in that environment. We continue to make progress on resolving lingering issues for a subset of the same-store SHOP portfolio where we believe capital could be more productively redeployed. We will provide further details as our plans are finalized.

We are pleased with this year's investment activity and the opportunities ahead. Our priority remains disciplined growth, acquiring communities at an attractive basis, expanding strategic operator relationships and investing where we see the strongest potential for long-term returns. With that, I'll turn the call over to Chris to discuss portfolio performance and our operating initiatives.

Christian Maingot: Thank you, Kevin. It's a privilege to join you today as NHI's Chief Operating Officer. I joined NHI because I believe the company is well positioned to be the capital partner of choice for senior housing operators. Its track record of building long-term relationships, seasoned team, access to capital and growing commitment to SHOP were key factors that attracted me to the company. Over the past 3 months, I've spent considerable time with our team and operating partners getting to know the portfolio. That work has only reinforced my confidence in the opportunity ahead. It has also helped us identify some near-term priorities and opportunities for improvement, which I'll touch on in a moment.

Let me start with our third quarter results. Total SHOP NOI increased 134% through significant contributions from acquisitions over the past 12 months, and the Same-Store portfolio grew NOI 19.5% year-over-year. While the growth was substantial, the quarterly result was below our expectations. Occupancy growth during the summer selling season was slower than we anticipated. It reflected transition-related operating disruptions and slower growth at certain legacy holiday communities. Unusually high onetime expenses at select operators also impacted the quarter. Our immediate priority is improving revenue growth with particular attention to communities where filling vacant units offers the greatest RevPAR opportunity.

We've been working with our operating partners to ensure they have in place fully staffed sales positions, rent-ready inventory, pricing and incentives that align with local market conditions. We also introduced targeted short-term incentives for community teams at select properties to support sales activity. The sequential increase in recurring capital expenditures largely reflected spending to prepare vacant units for occupancy. Having that inventory available is essential to converting tours to move-ins. As we execute on these properties, we see some encouraging signs in occupancy over the past 3 months. Consistent with typical seasonal patterns in senior housing, we expect occupancy to taper as we move through the holiday season.

Move-in incentives and variability in holiday-related expenses may also temper the near-term financial benefit of that progress. At the recently acquired communities, we view these transition-related disruptions as temporary. We continue to believe these investments can perform in line with our underwriting and are working to get performance on the right track. We are currently partnering with our operators to develop the 2027 operating budget and business plans. We entered that process with a clear view of the growth opportunities across the portfolio, the communities that warrant the greatest attention and the actions needed to improve performance. Our approach is tailored to each community and includes identifying revenue-enhancing capital projects and directing resources where we see the greatest potential return.

To support this work, we've also made some changes to our internal organization. We have realigned our asset management team by investment type and functional expertise, establishing separate teams dedicated to SHOP and to triple net and loans with clear responsibilities and expectations for each. Data-driven decision-making will be central to our approach. To support that commitment, we have bolstered our data and analytics team. This team will strengthen our portfolio insights while improving our tools, processes and technologies. By combining community operating data with broader market and customer information, we aim to identify opportunities earlier, work more efficiently and help our team and operating partners improve performance. We are recruiting for several positions to support these efforts.

Over time, I believe this approach can support organic SHOP NOI growth in the high single-digit to low double-digit range. Our priority is improving execution and establishing a more consistent operating trajectory. Turning to triple-net portfolio. The portfolio continues to provide a solid foundation with healthy EBITDARM coverage across the major asset classes. Bickford paid more than $1 million in percentage rent during the quarter, modestly ahead of our guidance assumptions. My first 3 months have only strengthened my confidence in NHI's opportunity. Our focus is on translating that opportunity into consistent operating performance as we grow, and I look forward to doing so alongside our team and operating partners.

With that, I'll turn the call over to Todd to discuss our financial results.

Todd Siefert: Thank you, Chris, and hello, everyone. Let me start by providing details on our third quarter financial results, followed by a brief discussion about our balance sheet and liquidity and conclude with our revised guidance. For the quarter ended September 30, 2026, our net income per share was $11.47 compared to $0.69 per share in the prior year third quarter. The significant increase was driven by a $541.6 million gain on the sale of the NHC portfolio. We also recognized $9.9 million impairment on a property that was held -- classified as held for sale during the quarter and has subsequently been sold in the fourth quarter.

NAREIT FFO and normalized FFO for the third quarter were $1.12 per share, respectively, compared to $1.09 and $1.32 per share, respectively, in the prior year period. NAREIT FFO and normalized FFO for the third quarter of 2026 included $1.1 million in transaction costs related to acquisitions we decided not to pursue. NAREIT FFO and normalized FFO in the third quarter of 2025 included approximately $3.9 million in cash rental income related to lease terminations on 7 properties previously leased to Discovery Senior Living that were not -- were part of our SHOP transition. The third quarter of 2025 also included approximately $9.7 million in rental income from NHC.

FAD for the third quarter decreased by approximately $5.2 million compared to the prior year period. The decrease was driven primarily by the sale of the NHC portfolio and the prior year's Discovery lease termination accounted for approximately $13.6 million of the decline. This was offset by acquisitions completed since the end of last year's third quarter. Our cash rental income of $58.4 million declined by $12.9 million compared to the prior year's third quarter due to the items I just reviewed and other dispositions partially offset by acquisitions completed over the past year. Total SHOP NOI increased by 134% to $11.5 million.

Interest income from mortgages and other notes declined by 20.6% to $5.1 million due to lower outstanding notes receivable balances. We also earned approximately $2.9 million of interest on NHC sale proceeds held for future Section 1031 exchanges. General and administrative expenses for the third quarter increased 26.8% to $8 million compared to $6.3 million in the third quarter last year as the company continues to ramp its SHOP growth strategy, which contributed to a $1 million increase in compensation costs. Legal expense of $1 million consisted primarily of costs incurred on a large SHOP transaction that we stopped pursuing during the quarter, as referenced earlier.

Interest expense for the third quarter decreased 9% year-over-year due to lower average debt outstanding. Turning to our balance sheet and liquidity. Our reported net debt to adjusted EBITDA ratio at September 30 was 3.6x. Adjusting for the $340 million available for future Section 1031 exchanges, our net debt to adjusted EBITDA was 2.4x. We have a $100 million private placement note due in January 2027, which we expect to repay by the end of 2026. We have no other debt maturities until 2028. Our available liquidity at September 30 was approximately $1 billion, consisting of $497 million of available revolver capacity, $500 million under our recently refreshed ATM program and cash on hand.

We also had approximately $340 million in restricted cash and deposits held for future qualified Section 1031 acquisitions at the end of the quarter. With the investments that we expect to close by the end of the year, we do not expect a special dividend will be required for federal income tax purposes. Regarding the quarterly dividend, our Board of Directors declared a quarterly dividend of $0.94 per share payable January 29, 2027, to stockholders of record on December 31, 2026. As announced last night, we revised our full year guidance to a range of $4.68 to $4.70 per share for both NAREIT and normalized NFFO and $236.6 million to $237.7 million for funds available for distribution.

The revised FAD range represents a $5 million reduction at the midpoint. The revised guidance incorporates approximately $2.3 million in reduced SHOP NOI from the 42 properties in the portfolio at September 30 and increased recurring CapEx of approximately $2.8 million. As Chris discussed, we accelerated certain routine capital expenditures that are supporting occupancy gains. These items are partially offset by stronger performance and expectations elsewhere in the organization compared to our prior guidance. That concludes our prepared remarks. Thank you again for joining us today. Operator, please open the line for questions.

Operator: [Operator Instructions] Your first question is coming from Juan Sanabria from BMO Capital Markets.

Juan Sanabria: Just hoping you could expand a little bit on what drove the SHOP underperformance relative to expectations? Was it smaller assets, older assets, any particular operators to call out? Is there any reliance on third-party referrals? Just hoping to dig a little bit under the surface just to get a better understanding.

Christian Maingot: Yes. This is Chris. First of all, great to be here on my first NHI call. Breaking that down a little bit, definitely, it was a revenue issue. I'll put it in 2 different buckets. As it relates to our new acquisitions, in those particular transactions, we did experience operator transitions. And with any operator transition, there tends to be some disruption. I think the disruption here was a little bit more pronounced. We saw some pretty local mom-and-pop type family businesses being sold to more sophisticated incoming operators. And with that, there's a level of complexity as you introduce new technologies and so forth. So the pickup there was a little slower than we expected.

We did see in a portfolio, the incoming occupancy was below where we thought the starting point would be. And then we saw in another portfolio just some immediate move-outs. Ultimately, and I've been to a number of these assets and portfolios, we believe in these deals. We think the long-term business plan is in line with our underwriting. Stefano Marchetti: "Cittadella merita rispetto, c'è stato levato qualcosa dagli episodi. Gol annullati pesano"

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

“Sono molto contento di ciò che stiamo facendo e per come stiamo interpretando il campionato. Mi fa piacere per i ragazzi, la squadra mi sta dando soddisfazioni”. Così a TuttoMercatoWeb.com il dg del Cittadella, Stefano Marchetti.

Direttore, siete primi in classifica. La scelta di aver affidato la panchina a Musso sta pagando. “Ha confermato di avere grandi capacità. È un uomo Cittadella, ha iniziato e smesso con noi. Tutta la sua vita è stata legata al Cittadella, conosco da sempre i suoi valori”.

Qual è l'obiettivo del Cittadella? “Vogliamo fare bene. Ultimamente però c’è stato levato qualcosa e non lo accetto. Qualche episodio arbitrale c’è andato contro, il Cittadella merita rispetto e uniformità di giudizio sul campo. I gol regolari annullati portano via punti e più di qualche episodio c’è andato contro. Bisogna porre maggiore attenzione agli episodi che alla fine spostano gli equilibri. Il Cittadella è una società seria che ha una storia e merita rispetto”.

Cosa intende per fare bene? “Parlare prima serve a poco. Gli obiettivi vanno centrati e non dichiarati. Fare bene vuol dire mettere le basi per arrivare a giocarci qualcosa di bello. Oggi però dirlo è troppo presto”.

Avete tanti elementi di valore, alcuni dei quali provenienti dal settore giovanile. Su tutti Ihnatov. “Abbiamo più di qualche ragazzo interesse. C’è un bel mix di giovani ed esperti. Ihnatov sta confermando le qualità che avevamo intravisto nel settore giovanile, merito anche del nostro responsabile Andrea Paolucci. Ma ne abbiamo anche altri: Gobbato, Gambaro, Zillio, Ceccarelli... tutti ragazzi che stanno crescendo confermando le buone qualità".

Presto sarà tempo di elezioni in FIGC. Malagò o Galliani, da che parte state? “Dobbiamo ancora confrontarci con il presidente e la società. Ci confronteremo e poi decideremo quale sarà la cosa migliore da scegliere. Prima andrà fatto un confronto sui programmi e le riforme”.

Sosta per Serie A e B: in che momento arriva? “È un bene per chi è in difficoltà che può trovare energie nuove. Diventa però un ostacolo per chi sta andando forte. Ma le soste vanno accettate: la Nazionale deve ritrovare i valori di un tempo e bisogna darle spazio. L’Italia ha una storia e un blasone che deve ritrovare. Non andiamo ai Mondiali da troppo e questo non fa bene al calcio”.

La sorpresa, oggi, è Gattuso con la sua Lazio. “Ci abbiamo giocato contro, personalmente non lo conosco ma ogni volta che lo vedo e sento parlare mi emoziona. Ha un’umanità e un modo di trasmettere le cose che mi emozionano. L’ho sentito parlare anche a Trento, mi da l’idea di essere un uomo vero che ha valori importanti. È stato un grande campione, dietro c’è anche una grande persona. Prima viene l’uomo e poi il tecnico: non mi stupisce ciò che sta facendo”.

Ex-60 Minutes EP On His Biggest Concerns Amid Bari Weiss’ Leadership: ‘That Would Have Freaked Me Out’

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

60 Minutes’ Lesley Stahl Addresses Backlash Over Her Decision To Stay Amid Bari Weiss’ Tenure

Bari Weiss Opens Up About Her Biggest Objective For CBS News: ‘That Is First And Foremost The Goal’

60 Minutes Alum On The Show’s Bari Weiss Era And One Thing She Still Finds ‘Disturbing’

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The show's new direction continues to draw feedback.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

60 Minutes has entered a new era amid the 2026 TV schedule, and a person who’s been overseeing much of the work behind the scenes is CBS News Editor-in-Chief Bari Weiss. In conjunction with the news magazine show’s EP, Nick Bilton, Weiss has been looking to update the program, and those efforts have been accompanied by major staff changes and backlash. Some of the criticisms have come from former staff members, including ex-EP Bill Owens, who recently shared his biggest concerns over the series he once shepherded.

Owens worked at CBS News for 37 years and, in 2019, he was named the EP of 60 Minutes after having spent 12 years overseeing its content in other roles. He ultimately resigned from his post in April 2025 amid a flurry of behind-the-scenes changes at 60. Since his departure, Owens has discussed his time with the show, and he notably shared thoughts ahead of the Season 59 premiere, which aired in September. At Semafor’s New Media & Marketing Playbook event, Owens was asked for his thoughts on his former workplace and said:

Not a lot. [Skydance owns] the company, they can do whatever they want. But the ratings are telling. There’s no doubt that linear television is in decline. But the numbers the other day were less than 6 million people. It’s like the evening news. 60 Minutes? That would have freaked me out.

Ratings for the latest season of 60 have reportedly been down compared to those of Season 58. The season premiere notched lower viewership and, despite some forward progress in subsequent weeks, more recent numbers haven’t been so strong. For instance, per data on the show’s October 25 broadcast, the episode only drew 5.6 million live viewers, which fell well below Season 58’s 9.1 million average. Despite that, when Bari Weiss addressed concerns, she stated that the show was doing “tremendously well.”

As for Owens, he’s been candid about leaving 60 Minutes, and he has attributed that move to no longer having the editorial independence he and his colleagues had long been granted. During the Semafor event, Owens also criticized the show’s interview with Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth and argued that better questions should’ve been asked. On a separate note, the veteran journalist also admitted that he wished his departure would’ve had a different effect on 60:

When I left, I really hoped that I was going to cause enough of a commotion that they would kind of back off. And the people, my senior team, the correspondents, they’ve tried their best. But it’s just a really difficult circumstance.

May 2026 marked the beginning of major personnel changes at 60 Minutes, as several high-profile staffers were let go, including correspondents Sharyn Alfonsi and Cecilia Vega as well as former EP Tanya Simon. Days later, fellow host Scott Pelley was fired as well after he reportedly accused Weiss of “murdering” the show. Around that time, Owens defended Pelley’s comments, saying that he “stood up the way that I did a year ago and I couldn’t be prouder of him, and I know all the people at 60 Minutes couldn’t be prouder of him.”

Bill Owens has since moved on to other ventures and, beginning in March 2027, he’ll executive-produce a YouTube-based outlet known as NewPress. It’s also been rumored that Owens will spill tea about his time at 60 Minutes through a tell-all memoir, but that’s yet to materialize. At the very least, though, Owens still seems somewhat interested about what the future holds for 60.

/C O R R E C T I O N - Supermetrics/

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In the news release, Supermetrics Launches Campaign Manager, Bringing AI-Powered Insights and Campaign Execution Together, issued 10-Nov-2026 by Supermetrics over PR Newswire, we are advised by the company that changes have been made. The complete, corrected release follows, with additional details at the end:

New AI-powered capabilities help marketers move from answers to action across advertising platforms, with trusted data, human oversight and control over campaign decisions

HELSINKI, Nov. 10, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Supermetrics, the marketing intelligence platform powering more than 200,000 businesses, today announced the launch of Campaign Management, a new solution designed to help marketers bridge the gap between understanding campaign performance and taking action with AI. By bringing cross-platform performance marketing data and campaign actions into AI chats, Supermetrics is helping businesses turn AI marketing recommendations into optimization actions with a simple in-chat approval.

For years, marketers have relied on increasingly sophisticated tools to understand campaign performance, yet acting on those insights often remains a fragmented, manual process. Teams must navigate multiple advertising platforms, reconcile disconnected data and translate recommendations into campaign changes, losing time and slowing their ability to respond to campaign issues and changing performance marketing priorities.

Campaign Management addresses this challenge by connecting marketing intelligence directly to campaign execution. Built on Supermetrics' established data infrastructure, the solution combines trusted, connected marketing data with AI-powered workflows, so marketers can identify opportunities, evaluate recommended changes and take action across advertising channels from whichever AI solution they currently use.

"There's pressure on every marketing team to put AI to work, but not everyone is starting from the same place," said Anssi Rusi, CEO of Supermetrics. "Some are still getting their data connected. Others are already using tools like Claude to analyze performance and work out what to do next. For those teams, the question is, 'Why do I have to leave this conversation to make the change?' That's who we're building Campaign Management for. They want to act on the recommendations where they're already working, while staying in control of what happens to their campaigns."

From Marketing Intelligence to Intelligent Action

Campaign Management represents the next step in Supermetrics' evolution from marketing data integration to AI-powered marketing intelligence and execution.

Rather than relying on isolated, platform-specific recommendations, Campaign Management is designed to help marketers make decisions informed by a broader view of their marketing performance and business priorities.

The solution introduces capabilities designed to help marketing teams:

Premier Cru Solutions, a six-person digital marketing agency serving premium and luxury wineries, is already saving around 30% of its time using Supermetrics Campaign Management and Supermetrics for Claude. The team builds, restructures and optimizes Google Ads and Meta campaigns from one chat, directing the saved time toward deeper client analysis. In one Google Search rebuild, the agency completed a campaign build estimated at 14 hours in two hours, before final fine-tuning.

"Getting our data into Claude with Supermetrics changed how we analyze," said Matthew Thompson, Founder and Head of Growth at Premier Cru Solutions. "Campaign Management is changing how we work. We find the problem and make the fix across Google and Meta from the same chat, and every change gets checked before it goes live. A Search build we estimated at 14 hours, took two. We aren't using that time to take on more clients. We're using the time and richer data to give our clients answers they may have never had before."

Building a More Connected Advertising Ecosystem

As advertising becomes increasingly complex, marketers need solutions that bring together data, intelligence and execution across the platforms where their campaigns operate.

Supermetrics' Campaign Management can manage campaigns across Google, Meta, LinkedIn, Microsoft Advertising,TikTok, Snapchat and ChatGPT Ads, reflecting the growing importance of trusted data connectivity and more actionable marketing intelligence.

"Snapchat is dedicated to helping businesses of all sizes reach new customers and drive growth. Our partnership with Supermetrics helps advertisers turn insights into action with the AI tools they already use, simplifying campaign management and making it easier to drive better results." â Alfie Villanueva Brandt, Senior Manager, Ad Product Partnerships, Snapchat

"At Microsoft Advertising, we believe AI has the ability to make marketing simpler, more connected and more actionable. Together with partners like Supermetrics, we're helping marketers turn trusted, connected data into better decisions and faster action, while keeping them in control" said Jason McKay, VP Partnerships, Microsoft Advertising.

"Agentic AI is transforming marketing and it requires an interconnected ecosystem for marketers to realize its full potential. The TikTok MCP Server enables developers and platforms to build AI solutions that connect seamlessly with TikTok, helping reduce complexity, move faster, and drive real business outcomes" said David Kaufman, Global Head of Monetization Product Partnerships, TikTok

The introduction of Campaign Management builds on Supermetrics' broader investment in making marketing data more accessible, actionable and AI-ready.

As AI becomes more deeply embedded in marketing workflows, the challenge for organizations is shifting from simply accessing information to ensuring AI-driven recommendations are grounded in reliable data, relevant business context and appropriate human oversight.

With Campaign Management, Supermetrics is extending its role beyond helping marketers understand performance to helping them act on that understanding.

The launch also builds on Supermetrics' recent advancements in AI-powered marketing workflows, including its integration with Claude, reinforcing the company's vision of a more connected marketing ecosystem in which AI can support the full journey from data analysis to informed action.

"AI will only be as valuable as the data and context behind it," added Rusi. "The future of marketing isn't about handing every decision over to AI. It's about giving marketers the intelligence, confidence and control to act at the speed their businesses demand. Campaign Management is an important step toward making that future a reality."

About SupermetricsSupermetrics is the Marketing Intelligence Platform that helps marketers bring together data from unlimited marketing and sales sources, get answers they can trust, and act on them directly from their preferred AI chat tool. More than 200,000 businesses worldwide use Supermetrics, including TBWA, Dentsu, Dyson, Heineken, AirFrance, Levi's and Deloitte. Founded in 2013 and headquartered in Helsinki, Finland, Supermetrics has 350 employees across Europe, Asia and North America.
