Financial institutions confront the complexities of scaling AI

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AI in financial services is moving beyond the pilot phase as banks, capital markets firms, mortgage lenders and other organizations begin deploying the technology more broadly. The challenge is shifting toward managing AI responsibly at scale while meeting strict requirements for security, compliance and customer trust.

The AWS Financial Services Symposium highlighted that transition in May. As financial institutions expand their use of AI, they have opportunities to test new applications without committing to large-scale deployments, according to Scott Mullins, former general manager of AWS Worldwide Financial Services at Amazon Web Services Inc.

“You can spin this stuff up really quickly; you can experiment with it,” he said during the symposium. “You can show that it actually achieves the things that you want to achieve within specific policy guidelines and frameworks, and you can actually evaluate whether that governance is holding.”

This feature is part of SiliconANGLE Media’s ongoing coverage of the AWS Financial Services Symposium. (* Disclosure below.)

Moving AI from experimentation into production requires financial institutions to address a different set of challenges. Beyond demonstrating that the technology works, organizations must establish the governance and operational safeguards needed to support AI across regulated business processes, according to Paul Nashawaty, practice lead and principal analyst for application development at theCUBE Research.

“Our AppDev research shows that 92% of organizations now integrate AI into at least one stage of the software delivery lifecycle, yet only about one-third have established the governance, security, and operational maturity required for production-scale AI,” he said. “In banking, capital markets and insurance, the gap is even more pronounced because compliance, explainability, and customer trust are non-negotiable. The next phase of AI adoption will be defined less by model performance and more by platforms that embed policy controls, auditability and security into AI-enabled workflows while improving customer experiences and operational efficiency.”

Infosys Ltd. is addressing those requirements through its AI platform, which incorporates safeguards and controls intended to help financial institutions deploy AI while managing risk, according to Rajaram R.K., vice president and head of sales for financial services at Infosys.

“It has the securities [and] it has the guardrails. It has the token economics … all the capabilities that an organization would need,” he told theCUBE.

Financial institutions face stringent regulatory requirements, making security, auditability and recovery essential to their operations. As banks, insurers and capital markets firms expand their use of AI, those requirements take on additional importance, particularly when automated systems begin influencing business decisions. Despite these challenges, financial institutions are among the sectors leading the way in AI adoption.

“The organizations that treat AI as a regulated business capability, not simply a technology initiative, will be best positioned to accelerate innovation while maintaining resilience and meeting evolving regulatory expectations,” Nashawaty said.

Applying those established risk-management practices to AI requires organizations to consider how automated systems access data, make decisions and operate within existing policies. The challenge is extending governance and operational controls to AI-enabled workflows without undermining the efficiency and innovation those systems are intended to deliver.

Financial institutions are finding practical applications for AI in customer service and employee productivity. At New York Life Insurance Co., reducing routine manual work is giving employees more time to provide personalized service and respond proactively to customers’ needs, according to Kristina Welke, vice president at New York Life.

“We really felt there was a lot of opportunity to support them, remove the manual work they do every day, enable them to provide that consultative support, be proactive versus reactive,” she told theCUBE.

Fifth Third Bank is using conversational AI to improve customer interactions and reduce average call-handling time. The bank is using Amazon Connect to support its customer-service operations, according to Kevin Anderson, product manager on the AI transformation team at Fifth Third Bank.

“Amazon Connect really lays the foundation as it solves for not only messaging but voice as well, allows for AI and automation,” he explained.

For financial institutions, scaling AI depends in part on modernizing the infrastructure that supports data-intensive workloads. Cloud platforms can provide the flexibility to deploy new capabilities while maintaining access to existing systems, making modernization an important consideration as organizations expand their AI initiatives.

Mortgage lender PennyMac Financial Services recently expanded its agreement with Amazon Web Services to accelerate modernization of its proprietary mortgage servicing platform, Plaisse. The partnership also includes plans to deploy enterprise-grade AI capabilities and further develop the platform.

As financial institutions modernize their infrastructure, they also need ways to move and manage data without disrupting existing operations. NetApp Inc. is addressing those requirements through storage and data-management capabilities designed to support migration, resilience and performance in AWS environments, according to Jacob Lim, cloud solutions architect at NetApp.

“We create value for customers by making it simple to migrate and run their data in AWS by combining proven NetApp ONTAP capabilities, such as space-efficient snapshots, near-instant data cloning capabilities with minimal storage overhead and storage performance,” he told theCUBE. “Then, there is also the highly efficient data replication and ransomware detection engine natively built into the operating system directly. Combined with AWS scale and elasticity, customers gained resilience, performance and operational simplicity in the cloud.”

Scaling AI in financial services brings new challenges

As financial institutions incorporate AI into daily operations, managing the complexity of those deployments is becoming an increasingly important challenge. According to PagerDuty’s 2026 State of AI-First Operations report, 59% of surveyed banks were actively incorporating AI into their operations, while another 34% were discussing implementation. As adoption expands, institutions must determine how to govern these systems, maintain data quality and integrate AI into existing workflows.

“I think you’re going to see AI really become: How do we govern, how do we use [and] how do we make sure the data’s clean? How does it become [about] helping us be more efficient in what we’re doing in day-in and day-out operations?” Todd McNabb, chief revenue officer of PagerDuty Inc., told theCUBE.

Technology providers are responding to the demand for faster enterprise AI deployment. AWS’ recently announced $1 billion investment in forward-deployed engineering, for example, is intended to help customers develop and deploy agentic AI applications more quickly. For financial institutions, however, the measure of success will extend beyond deployment speed to how effectively AI can operate within existing systems, regulatory requirements and business processes.

(* Disclosure: TheCUBE is a paid media partner for the AWS Financial Services Symposium event. Neither AWS, the sponsor of theCUBE’s event coverage, nor other sponsors have editorial control over content on theCUBE or SiliconANGLE.)

Our new proprietary theCUBE AI Video Cloud is breaking ground in audience interaction, leveraging theCUBEai.com neural network to help technology companies make data-driven decisions and stay at the forefront of industry conversations.

The 8 Best Beginner Snowboards of 2027

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

What to know: This guide covers the eight best snowboards for beginners, including the Editors’ Choice Salomon Lotus (women’s) and Salomon Pulse (men’s). Testers evaluated the boards based on how well they flex, carve, and hold an edge. Keep reading for our tips on choosing the best snowboard if you’re just starting out or are looking to progress.

Bunny hills might not be steep, but the snowboard learning curve definitely is. You’ve got to pay your dues and eat shit snow a couple times. But after a lesson or two and some good old-fashioned repetition, things will start to click.

One of the biggest mistakes beginners can make is riding the wrong equipment. We constantly see beginners riding boards that are too stiff, too big, or too small. That’s why we pulled together this guide: to help you find the right snowboard for you, so that the path from beginner to intermediate is a little smoother—and a little less painful.

This past spring, we put 44 snowboards to the test. We asked our crew of snowboard testers—including instructors and coaches—to identify boards that were easy to ride and approachable for beginners. Below, you’ll find the result of months of testing and countless laps in the Sierra, Wasatch, and Rockies: our favorite beginner snowboards of 2027.

Sizes: 142-163 cm (Pulse); 135-155 cm (Lotus) Genre: All-mountain

Pros and Cons + Versatile and fun + Beginner-friendly shape + Encourages learning fundamentals – Not ideal in choppy conditions – Overwhelmed by high speeds

If you’re looking for a snowboard that will help you learn solid fundamentals without encouraging bad habits, the Salomon Lotus (women’s) and Pulse (men’s) are hard to beat—and easy to ride. They’re soft-flexing and approachable, proving to be “exceptionally intuitive for any skill level,” according to a Tahoe tester.

Gentle camber underfoot supplies grip in firmer snow, and a touch of pop makes it approachable to learn how to ollie and cruise through the park. The Pulse and Lotus are also directional twins, with a bumped back stance for float in powder and stability at speed, while the nose and tail are the same width for solid switch handling.

In slush and hardpack, one tester appreciated the camber underfoot, which flows into a rockered nose and tail. “You would have to try very hard to catch an edge. This is a confidence-building board,” she reported. The Pulse is similarly predictable, stable, and easy to manipulate.

The Salomon decks showed their weaknesses at high speeds, on bumpy, icy snow, and in technical moguls—terrain and conditions most beginners will want to avoid anyway. For newer riders, though, this board is an ideal first buy.

Sizes: 146-161W cm Genre: All-mountain freestyle

Pros and Cons + Easy to turn + Buttery, playful for stepping into the terrain park + Rides switch well – Soft flex and rocker-dominant profile make it less of a long-term investment

For a solid beginner board that doesn’t break the bank, the Rossignol Ampage Vol. 2 is an easy-riding, rocker-dominant, twin shape that rides regular and switch equally well.

The Ampage is soft—way too soft for riding hard off-piste—but the flex is perfect for learning the ropes, practicing on groomers, and getting a feel for buttering, jibbing, and jumping in small terrain parks. “Both the nose and tail are flexible for buttering,” a Tahoe tester reported, speaking to the Ampage’s inherent playful nature. The rocker is designed to make turns feel smooth and catch-free, while a touch of camber adds moderate grip while carving.

Even at full retail ($400), the Ampage is a decent deal. But last year’s version is currently on sale, making it an incredible steal for boarders on a budget. Because of the core malleability and easy-riding rocker profile, progressing beginners might outgrow this board more quickly than other decks on this list.

Sizes: 150-167W cm Genre: All-mountain/Freeride

Pros and Cons + Floaty freeride shape + Playful freestyle chops – Not as reliable straightlining – Undergunned in chop

The redesigned Jones Frontier 2.0 is softer, more buoyant, and friendlier than ever, making it a solid option for powder-chasing beginners, intermediate riders learning to navigate the deep stuff, or cruisy shredders who appreciate surfy, laidback turns.

The exaggerated, elongated, rockered nose and a touch of taper (8 millimeters) help the Frontier perform well in powder, while three-dimensional contouring facilitates smooth and natural carving. Camber beneath your boots, a medium sidecut, and Jones’ serrated Traction Tech edges help the shape bite into piste on rail. “It lays sweet, soft trenches, and allows for nuanced wiggling underfoot,” applauded a hard-to-please Colorado tester who was surprised by how much he enjoyed the Frontier’s cruise control.

An approachable combo of biaxial fiberglass and a poplar and paulownia core yields a softer, predictable flex pattern. Testers had mixed feedback on the resulting ride. Some found the Frontier 2.0 “effortless and forgiving.” Multiple harder-charging riders called it a “dad cruiser,” with a 30-year-old ripper joking he’d happily buy it—in 20 years. But for beginners and intermediates especially, that flex makes for an intuitive, easy-to-direct deck that facilitates flowy turns and forgiving forays into powder.

Sizes: 151-170W cm (men’s); 139-154 cm (women’s) Genre: All-mountain freestyle

Pros and Cons + Approachable flex + Decent float in powder yet still solid riding switch + More butter than Land O’Lakes 𑂾 Overwhelmed at high speeds in variable conditions 𑂾 Not the most reliable for bigger drops

The Jones Rally Cat combines a capable, all-mountain directional twin shape (a touch of setback, zero taper, full camber, and spooned-out nose and tail) with a softer, forgiving flex pattern. The pliable feel comes from a poplar/paulownia wood core and a dual-direction, dual-layer, stitched fiberglass laminate.

A progressing, all-mountain Tahoe tester judged the flex “loose enough to be playful but stiff enough to hold an edge.” The shape inspired confidence and elevated her riding to a “more fun level,” and she recommended the board for “new riders, park riders, and playful freeriders.”

Despite its setback stance, the Rally Cat’s lack of taper made it equally adept at jibbing through the park and riding switch, according to one Mammoth snowboard coach. She also dug the tight, 6.6-meter sidecut for the 148-centimeter length we tested. Combined with the spoon-shaped nose and tail, she said the Rally Cat facilitates “effortless turn initiation and quick and efficient edge-to-edge transitions.”

However, a harder-charging North Lake Tahoe freerider and snowboard instructor warned that the buttery flex in the tail that makes the Rally Cat a blast on side hits and park laps can be overwhelmed by overhead drops and chop. This won’t be an issue for true beginners, but worth keeping in mind as you progress.

Sizes: 151-164X cm (men’s); 139-151 cm (women’s) Genre: All-mountain

Pros and Cons + Versatile twin + Most upside for progression out of any board in this guide + Durable build – Expensive – Too much for true beginners

Never Summer’s Proto Type 3 is more expensive and stiffer than the other options in this guide, but it’s also more stable and reliable, providing excellent edgehold, carving chops, and a solid backbone for handling sizeable jumps, cliff drops, and higher speeds. It’s too much for true beginners, but it can be a smart investment for late-stage beginners committed to progression and who want a board they can use for years to come.

The all-mountain twin features a classic Never Summer camber concoction, utilizing three camber bumps running from inserts to inserts, with rocker at the nose and tail. Combined with an adaptable sidecut and a medium-flexing, carbon-bolstered poplar-paulownia-birch core, the Proto Type 3 rips on edge. “Edgehold feels very locked in,” said a Tahoe tester. “Incredibly balanced and easy to shift from toe to heel,” commented a Mammoth rider who appreciated the Proto’s ability to tackle smaller to larger turns well.

The carbon reinforcements help the Proto Type 3 pop aggressively off lips like pubescent pimples. “Stable through the air even on the Palisades Gold Coast jump line,” commented a Tahoe freestyler who also praised the camber for keeping him upright on tail-heavy landings and the rubber laminates and UHMW sidewalls for dampening. The Mammoth rider agreed, saying the board smoothed out bumps that typically irritate her bad knees. Her verdict:  “After you’ve mastered making it down a green run, this board would be great for a late beginner to intermediate rider looking to perfect carving and increase confidence.”

Pros and Cons + Wide ride is perfect for riders with bigger boots + Turns confidently and floats well + Solid freestyle flavor – Can be sluggish compared to narrower boards

The Cardiff Lynx is a wide ride that’s a game-changer for boarders with bigger boots who have a penchant for powder. However, it’s more suited to progressing, late-stage beginners and aspiring intermediates than day-one newbies.

The generous width combined with rocker in the nose and tail, as well as a touch of taper, help the Lynx float extremely well in powder. A versatile, blended sidecut enables the Lynx to tackle a variety of turn sizes for true all-mountain capability, while subtle serrations along the edges help grip firm snow like the claws of the Cardiff’s namesake. “Edges lock into turns,” voiced a Tahoe snowboard instructor. “High-speed carving is fun.”

The Lynx is Cardiff’s softest offering, with a pliable cedar and aspen core that can handle rails, boxes, butters, and playful small-to-medium jumps. One Tahoe tester loved taking the board all over the mountain with a freestyle approach, noting that it handled switch riding better than expected for a tapered shape with a setback stance.

That said, the flex is stiffer and ultimately more capable in steeper all-mountain and freeride terrain than many of the other decks in this guide, although the Lynx’s build is not the most shock-absorbing in chunky moguls. Not to mention, that width can make the Lynx sluggish at slower speeds. But no one took issue with the cool graphics— testers loved the black, big cat-emblazoned topsheet.

Fit Tip: To make sure the Lynx will work for your boots, check the technical specs. In particular, look at the underfoot width at the front and rear foot. Compare that to the length of your boot sole, remembering that your boots may be turned slightly at an angle, depending on your stance preferences.

Sizes: 148-161W cm Genre: All-mountain freestyle

Pros and Cons + Approachable all-mountain cruiser + Excels hitting rails and boxes + Fun, buttery, and easy to maneuver – A bit soft for high-speed all-mountain ripping and freeriding

If you’ve already fallen in love with sliding boxes and rails, CAPiTA’s Outerspace Living might be a perfect fit. This directional twin rides switch well, with a sliver of setback for all-mountain riding en route to the park, so it can easily be your daily driver. It features gentle camber underfoot for pop and hardpack proficiency. Rocker in the nose and tail reduce common catch points while carving and facilitate easy jibbing and buttering. “It’s forgiving and light,” commented a Tahoe park regular. “You won’t catch an edge easily.”

The board’s minimal weight comes courtesy of a slender, malleable poplar and beech core. Despite carbon strips running parallel down the length of the deck, it rides on the softer side. Our testers loved that flex while hitting rails, spinning lazily off rollers, and sniffing out side hits, but our most advanced riders overpowered the Outerspace Living at high speeds in steeper terrain. It’s a great tool for playful all-mountain riders looking to develop their jib skills, or progressing riders looking for a stepping stone to more advanced boards.

Sizes: 135-162W cm Genre: All-mountain, park, powder

Pros and Cons + Great pop and landing gear for jumps + Decent jibber + Rides switch well – Too much for true beginners – Not our favorite in powder

When one of our crew test-drives a deck and instantly buys one for themselves, it’s a damn good sign. And that’s precisely what happened when one of our Mammoth testers put the Burton Good Company to use in her local terrain park. A true twin with camber underfoot and a slightly stiffer-than-advertised medium flex, this is a board that loves to snap ollies, power off lips, and stomp landings with authority.

“Handled medium-sized jumps like a dream,” reported that Mammoth rider, noting she felt confident and comfortable in the jump line. “Riding switch was a breeze,” voiced another Mammoth tester, who appreciated the Good Company’s grip on edge, which translated to boosting prowess in the mini pipe, too. She was also pleased by the board’s ability to spin on and off rails.

Because the Good Company felt a little stiffer than advertised, testers said it’s better for more advanced beginners or intermediates interested in park jumps. It’s also more capable than anticipated in all-mountain terrain, preferring medium and large radius turns to quicker, tighter ones. It doesn’t have a penchant for buttering, and it’s not our top pick for powder. But for progressing all-mountain riders, the Good Company delivers at a very fair price.

Shopping for a snowboard can be overwhelming, especially as a beginner. We’re not going to dive too deep on snowboard tech here (you can check out our additional snowboard coverage here for that), but we want to quickly help you understand a few key concepts discussed in this guide.

“Beginner” is a broad and admittedly unhelpful phrase. If you’re a true first-timer or close to it, you should focus on the first four snowboards in this guide. The best beginner snowboards are on the softer side, making them more approachable and easier to learn the basics on. They’re also generally a little cheaper than the next four boards we’ve included in this guide, which are boards we recommend for more experienced beginners, those approaching intermediate status, or riders who are hellbent on progression or excited to explore a certain style of riding.

Snowboards come in all shapes and sizes, but they can be considered true twins, directional boards, or directional twins.

True Twins, like the Rossignol Ampage, have a symmetrical nose and tail, which look the same and have the same flex. This ensures that the board feels the same whether you’re riding regular or switch. Twins are popular with park riders, as riding switch is a keystone of freestyle snowboarding. True twins are also a sweet choice for beginners, as they make learning “falling leaf” down steeper slopes a piece of cake.

Directional snowboards, like the Jones Frontier 2.0, have a purpose-built nose and tail. They’re designed to be ridden primarily in one direction, with the nose pointing downhill. They often have bigger, floatier noses and shorter, stubbier tails, a combination that can supply stability at speed, buoyancy in deep snow, and landing prowess. Directional snowboards often have taper, which means the nose is wider than the tail, and a setback stance that is bumped toward the back of the board. Both setback and taper can contribute to float and stability.

The main downside of directional boards is that they don’t ride switch nearly as well, which explains why many freeriders and all-mountain riders prefer directional boards, but freestylers stick to twins.

Directional twins combine certain elements from twin boards and others from directional boards to create hybrids that perform across a variety of conditions. For instance, the Jones Rally Cat is a directional twin in the sense that it features a nose and tail that are exactly the same width, enabling it to ride switch well, like a twin. But the nose is longer than the tail, helping the Rally Cat to float in powder and tackle steeper terrain like a directional board.

When you lay a snowboard flat on the ground and look at it from the side, you’re getting a feel for its profile. Profile dictates much of a snowboard’s riding qualities, as it influences how you weight, flex, and engage the board’s sidecut on snow.

Cambered snowboards, like the Burton Good Company Camber, look like an upside-down “U” when you lay them flat on the ground. There’s a raised central section between your feet, and two points of contact with the ground toward the nose and tail. Camber provides grip when you’re carving on firm snow, reliability and edgehold in technical terrain, and power when you’re snapping ollies. For some beginners, camber can be a touch trickier to learn on, and  can feel catchier than rocker. However, camber also helps snowboarders learn fundamentals the right way. You can’t cheat on a camber snowboard, whereas a rocker board is more forgiving and can subsequently let snowboarders develop sloppy habits. For that reason, we mostly recommend camber-dominant shapes in this guide, like our top pick, the Salomon Pulse and Lotus.

Rocker is the opposite of camber. The “U” is right side up this time, as rockered boards have one central point of contact with the ground between the feet, and the nose and tail are raised off the ground. Rocker-dominant boards, like the Rossignol Ampage Vol. 2, are less catchy than their camber counterparts, and they’re generally more playful, easier to butter, and more buoyant. However, they’re also less stable and don’t ollie as well.

Just like directional twins combine aspects from twins and directional shapes in search of a balanced ride, hybrid profiles merge attributes of camber and rocker. For instance, the CAPiTA Outerspace Living pairs grippy camber underfoot with catch-free, floaty rocker in the nose and tail.

Now that you’ve narrowed in on the board you want, you face an equally important question: What size should you get? Sizing a snowboard correctly can make or break your season, but the truth is that there’s no perfect formula. Here are a few things to consider to make the best possible choice for you.

Even as a beginner, you can learn about your preferences from snowboards you’ve already ridden. For instance, if you rented a 155 and it felt too squirrely and unstable, you might want to size up a touch. If you demoed a 160 and it felt too beefy and you weren’t able to turn it, sizing down is a smart idea. Especially as you’re progressing from a true beginner towards intermediate status, it’s important to demo lots of snowboards, as it’ll help you develop your personal taste and your snowboard vocabulary.

Always pay close attention to specs provided by manufacturers, as many brands provide helpful sizing parameters, including ranges for rider weight, boot size, and height. Use this information as a potential starting point.

Between two sizes? If you’re a beginner or riding park, gravitate toward the smaller size, which are easier to turn and spin. If you’re getting more advanced or you’re riding powder frequently, consider the bigger size, which provides more float and stability.

Some brands won’t give you a range of rider heights, but they will give you a range of rider weights, as weight influences how a rider engages flex and how much a board will float. For example, there are skinny 6’3” riders who like 158s, and squat, powerful 5’8” riders who dig 160s.

If you have average-sized feet for your height and weight, you don’t need to worry too much about boot compatibility. But if you have big feet—say, you’re 5’9” and 150 pounds, but you have a size 11.5 boot—it’s important to consider wider snowboards like the Cardiff Lynx. Wide boards help riders with big feet avoid dragging their toes in the snow, which is at best annoying and at worst can cause avoidable crashes. When you scan those manufacturer-provided specs, pay attention to the width and, if possible, ideal boot size ranges. It’s also worth noting that certain snowboards, like the Never Summer Proto Type 3, are available in standard sizes, as well as wide sizes, which Never Summer denotes with an “X.”

This past spring, we sent 44 snowboards out to testers across the Wasatch, Rockies, and Sierra. Those testers aren’t beginners—far from it. These are advanced to expert riders who live and breathe snowboarding, with a few progressing intermediates to mix things up. This crew is intimately familiar with snowboard design concepts, and many of them have been testing snowboards for a decade. A handful of testers are currently instructors or coaches, or they’ve held those jobs in the past. Needless to say, they know what beginners need under their boots to learn the ropes.

After testing snowboards at their home mountains all spring, those testers filled out detailed snowboard review forms. They scored boards quantitatively on aspects like flex, responsiveness, pop, playfulness, stability at speed, etcetera. Then they dove deep on additional qualitative questions, among them: Would you spend your hard-earned money on this board? What conditions or terrain does this board excel or struggle in?

And then, most importantly, we ask these testers to rewind the clock and think like a beginner. Will the board in question encourage a beginner to progress and step up their game? Is it easy to ride? Or is it a hard-to-command bronco that’ll buck a less experienced snowboarder?

Finally, we sifted through those reviews, compared our favorite snowboards of 2027 to old favorites from last year, and compiled the guide you just finished reading. Our hope is that this labor of love helps you enjoy your time on the slopes. Happy hunting—and happier shredding!

Drew Zieff is a Tahoe-based freelance writer and a lifelong snowboarder. He directs Outside’s snowboard test, a role he’s handled since 2016. He also directs Backcountry Magazine’s splitboard coverage and nerds out on snowboard gear and travel for Whitelines Snowboarding, The Snowboarder’s Journal, REI, Gear Junkie, among others. He spends his winters testing gear in his backyard backcountry zones or up at Palisades when he’s not chasing stories and storms around the globe.

In a word, Kordell Black is a character. An esoteric ripper and veteran gear tester based in the Wasatch, Black has traveled the world with his snowboard in pursuit of powder and off-the-beaten-track adventures. He approaches big mountain lines with creative, freestyle energy, specializes in finding powder stashes overlooked by the Utah crowds, and thrashes boots into oblivion in a matter of weeks. He will also obliterate you in a flat ground game of SKATE.

Truckee’s own Jenna Shlachter is as brilliant on the hill as she is off it—which is saying something since she’s a Doctor of Psychology, after all. When she’s not on the clock, she’s usually on her snowboard, ripping everything from park to powder. Her trophy shelf needs regular reinforcement, as every year she seems to add banked slalom awards to her already impressive collection. Lucky for us, Shlachter is also an unabashed snowboard nerd, and she’s been a key contributor to our snowboard test for the last few seasons.

Western Sydney International Airport Opens After Bechtel-Led Delivery Program Completes Major Construction Works Seven Months Early

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Australia's first major greenfield international airport in more than 50 years delivered ahead of schedule: a role model for future infrastructure delivery in Australia

SYDNEY, Nov. 10, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Western Sydney International (Nancy-Bird Walton) Airport (WSI) opened to passengers on October 25, 2026, following a design, construction, and commissioning program led by Western Sydney Airport Corporation with Bechtel Australia as Delivery Partner that finished months ahead of schedule. The opening marks a new era of aviation, trade, and economic activity for Western Sydney and Australia.

Jetstar operated the airport's first commercial passenger service, flying from WSI to the Gold Coast. Passenger operations follow the start of freight services in July and cap 15 years of planning, seven years of construction, and a year of testing and operational readiness.

"I offer my warm congratulations to our partner, Western Sydney Airport Corporation, our contractors, and all the colleagues and partners who have come together to make this project such a success," said Darren Mort President of Infrastructure and Executive Director at Bechtel. "WSI shows what the integrated model can achieve when a customer, delivery partner, and contractors work as one team around a clear vision. Together, we transformed a 1,780-hectare greenfield site into a modern international airport, months ahead of schedule."

Since 2018, Bechtel has served as Delivery Partner to Western Sydney Airport Corporation, the company responsible for building and operating WSI. Under this model, Bechtel:

The program combined large-scale engineering with new approaches to sustainability and technology. It delivered Australia's largest earthworks program and reused nearly 6 million tonnes of sandstone from Sydney tunnelling projects that would otherwise have gone to landfill. More than 60 software platforms were integrated into one operating environment covering passenger processing, security, retail, and office systems. A 4.5-megawatt solar system, all-electric heating, and rainwater harvesting are built into the airport. And the new terminal includes extensive innovative elements, from the integrated domestic/international check-in area, a state-of-the art baggage system, and departure/arrival gates able to be used for both domestic and international flights.

The airport is designed to initially serve up to 10 million passengers a year, with capacity to expand as demand grows. It has already helped catalyze AU$19 billion in surrounding infrastructure investment and is projected to contribute AU$23.9 billion to Australia's gross domestic product by 2060.

Building on Bechtel's Commitment to Australia

"Today marks years of hard work, collaboration, and commitment from thousands of people, including more than 330 Bechtel colleagues across design, engineering, procurement, and project delivery," said Richard Freer, Managing Director of Bechtel's Public Infrastructure Business in Australia. "Excellent project set up, led by the Federal Infrastructure Department, strong front-end planning, disciplined project controls, and new ways of integrating complex systems kept multiple contractors and interconnected packages moving towards the same completion date. That's how we finished ahead of the schedule set back in 2018. Just as important, more than half of the workforce came from Western Sydney, so this airport was built by the community it now serves."

Bechtel has been serving Australian communities and customers for more than 70 years with projects spanning airports, rail, mines, mineral processing facilities, LNG installations, refineries, chemical plants, power stations, and telecommunications infrastructure. At peak construction on WSI, the project team brought together around 4,000 people; across the full construction period, more than 11,000 people worked on the project, with more than half drawn from Western Sydney. The program also engaged more than 360 local businesses and exceeded its workforce participation targets for local, diverse, First Nations and learner workers.

WSI is Australia's new 24-hour global gateway, providing domestic, international, and freight services, supporting Western Sydney's long-term growth and Australia's future aviation capacity.

Bechtel is a trusted engineering, construction, procurement, and project management partner to industry and government. Differentiated by the quality of our people and our relentless drive to deliver the most successful outcomes, we align our capabilities to our customers' objectives to create a lasting positive impact. Since 1898, we have helped customers complete more than 25,000 projects in 160 countries on all seven continents that have created jobs, grown economies, improved the resiliency of the world's infrastructure, increased access to energy, resources and vital services, and made the world a safer, cleaner place.

Bechtel serves the Energy; Infrastructure; Manufacturing & Technology; Mining & Metals; and Nuclear, Security & Environmental markets. Our services span from initial planning and investment through start-up and operations.

3 Easy Hacks To Make Instant Mashed Potatoes WAY Better

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

You might prefer homemade mashed potatoes, but sometimes you just need a shortcut—and on days like that, grabbing the box is WAY easier. But while the back-of-the-box directions will yield just-fine results, a few easy hacks will give you much better results.

As a recipe developer and professional cook, I don’t often make instant mashed potatoes, but I was excited by the challenge of leveling them up—without adding a bunch of extra ingredients or steps that take the convenience out of this convenience food. So I tried making instant mashed potatoes every way I could think of (including the way the box says!). In the end, I found three tricks that, while super simple, massively improve that final bowl of mashed potatoes.

Here are three ways to instantly upgrade instant mashed potatoes.

Most boxes tell you to rehydrate instant mashed potato flakes in hot water enriched with a little butter and a few tablespoons of milk—but I’m going all in on the milk here. The extra dairy really fills out the flavor of the mashed potato and makes it creamier—especially if you’re using a higher-fat milk, like whole or 2%.

When swapping milk for water, it’s best to increase the liquid amount just slightly. For some reason (totally scientific, I’m sure!), the potato flakes require more milk than water to hydrate properly. I recommend 2 extra tablespoons for every half cup of instant mashed potatoes.

Also, add the extra milk the box typically calls for. If you leave it out, the potatoes will be dry. When I was testing, I found it didn’t really matter when that second amount of milk went into the pan (at the beginning versus just before the potatoes), but some boxes emphasize putting it in cold just before adding the potatoes—so if you want to do that to make you feel safer, be my guest!

All the instant mashed potatoes I made lacked seasoning—until I doubled the salt. Then they were perfect. Now here’s the thing: I was testing with Diamond Crystal kosher salt, which is less salty by volume than table salt or Morton’s kosher. The back of the instant mashed potato box didn’t specify what type of salt they used, so if they tested with a fine salt or Morton’s kosher, maybe ¼ teaspoon was the perfect amount. But in my kitchen with Diamond Crystal, it was woefully underseasoned.

Maybe you’re nervous about oversalting and don’t want to double or even slightly increase the salt right away. But regardless, I’m begging you: Taste those potatoes before you serve them and don’t feel bad if you have to add another hefty pinch.

You might assume that mashed potatoes should be made in a pot—I definitely did. But the fluffiest, creamiest instant potatoes I made in this whole testing process came from a wide, high-sided sauté pan. The reason for this? Absolute science.

When adding the instant potato flakes to the hot liquid, the goal is to distribute them as evenly as possible without working them too much. Yes, the back of the box says to “stir” the potatoes in, but in testing, I quickly discovered that even a few strokes can quickly lead to gummy potatoes.

Here’s where the pan size comes in. Using a wide pan increases the liquid's surface area, so you can sprinkle the potatoes evenly over the top and pat them down into the liquid instead of stirring. From there, let them stand until they’ve absorbed the liquid, then give them a few stirs. Trust me: They’ll be way fluffier because you messed with them less!

Resident Evil remakes skipped the first game because Capcom worried it "might not resonate"

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"We felt that experiencing that was the best way to introduce the origins"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Capcom's modern Resident Evil remakes are fantastic entry points for one of the most beloved survival horror series of all time, but it's always felt weird that the very first 1996 game still hasn't been given the full-blown third-person RE Engine treatment. Yes, there's a remake from 2002, but it's way more faithful to the original game than the much more recent remakes of Resident Evil 2, 3, and 4, featuring the classic tank controls and static camera from the series' origin. Basically, if you want to start Resident Evil with the first game in the series, you have to deal with quite a few design foundations that'll feel seriously dated if you're unfamiliar. Like ink ribbons. You don't want ink ribbons, trust me.

With how successful the Resident Evil remakes have been, it may seem inevitable that Capcom revisits the very first game. But while rumors suggest there's already a new RE1 early in development, there's been no official announcement or even heavily substantiated reporting to add weight to those rumors. As to why Capcom didn't choose Resident Evil as the obvious starting point for its newer remakes, longtime series producer Jun Takeuchi says the company worried it wouldn't resonate with a modern audience.

"The reason we didn't choose Resident Evil 1 as the first remake is because it's such a stoic game," Takeuchi says in a new book celebrating the series' 30th anniversary (translation via Fandom). "Because it focuses so heavily on the horror derived from its stoicism, we were concerned that it might not resonate with today's game fans."

As is always the case with second-hand translations, it's hard to pin down the exact intent behind translated words like "stoic," but Resident Evil 1 definitely has a distinct vibe and slower pace than newer games in the series. Speaking of which, Takeuchi says the iconic opening sequence from Resident Evil 2, which sets up the mass infection of Raccoon City, is basically where everything kicks off anyway.

"Furthermore, the Raccoon City incident is essentially the starting point of the Resident Evil saga, so we felt that experiencing that was the best way to introduce the origins," he says.

I definitely don't disagree. Resident Evil 2 is arguably the most classic version of Resident Evil you can experience, whether that's the 1998 original or the 2019 remake. That said, as a lifelong fan and noted tank control hater, I would love to see a modern reimagining of the game that started it all.

Where does your favorite land on our ranking of the best Resident Evil games?

That means I'm responsible for managing the site's western regional executive branch, AKA my home office, and writing about whatever horror game I'm too afraid to finish.

Zendesk moves to pay-per-result AI pricing with its new Outcome AI Platform

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Customers will pay when AI resolves an issue, and a new directory helps AI assistants find official support.

Zendesk is changing how businesses pay for its AI, charging them for problems solved rather than for the number of people using the software. The customer service company announced the change on Tuesday at its AI Summit in San Francisco, as part of a new Outcome AI Platform.

Under the new plans, customers set an ‘Outcome Budget’ and pay for each eligible issue the AI resolves or task it completes. Zendesk checks each result for quality and completeness, so customers can see what counts towards their budget. As a result, businesses can start small and spend more as the AI takes on more work.

Zendesk has backed this model before. Earlier this year, it argued alongside Sierra that AI agents should be paid per result, not per seat, and OpenAI has started trying a similar approach with some customers.

Tapan Patel, a research director at IDC, said the change reflects how business buyers now want to pay for AI. Linking cost to verified results, he added, makes it clearer what customers get for their money.

The platform is also built for a world where people send their own AI assistants to deal with companies. To help with that, Zendesk is launching the Open Service Directory, a public list where brands can register their official support channels. AI assistants can then use it to find verified help, even for brands that do not use Zendesk.

Brands control what they share and when a human must step in. The directory does not access private customer data or take any action itself. Early members include Betterment, ElevenLabs and Telekom Payment.

Meanwhile, a tool called Zendesk Bridge lets businesses connect AI agents and apps of their choice, such as ChatGPT, to Zendesk’s systems. Setting up a connection starts with a name, which is kept for audit logs, and a plain description of what the outside agent should and should not do.

Administrators then choose exactly what each agent may do, such as reading, creating, answering, updating or merging tickets. An assistant called Admin Copilot suggests which tools to switch on, based on the description.

Every action then passes through Bridge, which checks permissions and company rules and asks for human approval where needed. An observability page shows how many requests came from AI agents and how many from people, which ones were blocked and why. It also includes a kill switch. Bridge is in early access, and the directory is launching soon.

“People don’t think about which channels, systems, or departments they need to navigate to interact with a business,” said Zendesk chief executive Tom Eggemeier.

Other new tools, now generally available, help teams build and improve their AI agents. AI Agent Launchpad studies past customer conversations and suggests where AI could take over. In Zendesk’s demo for a scooter rental firm, it proposes a step-by-step process for rides that will not end, using a connected billing system such as Stripe.

Similarly, Agentic Analytics lets managers ask questions about their data in plain language, such as how many tickets are still open. They can then see whether changes are cutting repeat contacts.

The launch reflects changing habits, according to a Zendesk survey. It found that 52% of consumers are comfortable relying on personal AI assistants for everyday tasks. Even so, 84% said human help should always be available.

The announcement follows Zendesk’s purchase of Forethought, its biggest deal in two decades, as it builds out its AI agents.

Anthony Mackie, Ariana DeBose, and John Leguizamo reveal why they joined Andy Weir's sci-fi audio drama 'Exoplanet' (interview)

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Latest space missions, discoveries and exploration updates.

Stargazing tips, cosmic events and expert insights on the universe

Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Sign up to our monthly entertainment newsletter to keep up with all our coverage of the latest sci-fi and space movies, tv shows, games and books.

Discover this week's must-see night sky events, moon phases, and stunning astrophotos. Sign up for our skywatching newsletter and explore the universe with us!

Space.com's Sci-Fi Reader's Club. Read a sci-fi short story every month and join a virtual community of fellow science fiction fans!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

'It's coming from brilliant minds who build worlds so impeccably. And it's about real stuff we’re dealing with today.'

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In any respectable audiobook project, finding the names of Andy Weir ("The Martian," "Project Hail Mary") and Benjamin Percy ("The Ninth Metal," "Wolverine") attached would elicit intense fan interest, but add the superstar vocal talents of Anthony Mackie ("Avengers: Infinity War," "Avengers: Doomsday"), Ariana DeBose ("West Side Story"), and John Leguizamo ("Ice Age"), and it catapults this sonic odyssey into the stratosphere.

Such is the case with "Exoplanet," Audible Original’s new 7-episode science fiction audio drama executive produced by Weir and penned by Percy. Its planet-hopping space disaster narrative sees the crew of the starship Wild Blue Yonder hit a quantum anomaly, forcing their ship to crash-land onto a deadly exoplanet. All is not as it seems, though, as each crew member arrives in a different era on the same world.

We hooked up with Mackie, DeBose, and Lequizamo to learn more about this renaissance of audio dramas and what attracted them to board Weir's sci-fi thriller.

One crew. One planet. Three timelines. No one survives alone. A sci-fi thriller executive produced by Andy Weir (#1 bestselling author of Project Hail Mary, The Martian), with an all-star cast led by Anthony Mackie, Ariana DeBose and John Leguizamo.

Exoplanet is free for Audible members, but also available for purchase separately.

Mackie voices Chief Engineer Jeff Shaw. His attraction to the imaginative genre began at an early age thanks to some cool family friends while growing up in the Big Easy.

"My brother went to college at Morehouse," he tells SPACE. "I was like 8, and he was 18. He’d always bring friends home, because New Orleans is a party city, so they'd come over and get drunk. But one of his friends gave me a book by Walter Mosley and one by Octavia Butler, and I got hooked into sci-fi and the future."

"From 'Back to the Future' to 'Aliens,' the future was such an idea of what was going to be out there. Now we’re living it," reveals Mackie. "It’s very important to me as a human being to have that construct as a part of my career. The only thing more arrogant than wanting to be president is standing up and saying, 'Yeah, I can go to space.' That sounds crazy to me."

Leguizamo's New York City childhood was influenced by absorbing classic radio dramas of the past, which primed him for this special Audible project.

“It was Anthony and Ariana being in it that really pumped me up, and of course Andy Weir," he recalls regarding his portrayal of Science Officer Logan Bowers. "Then when I got the theme, that it was something relevant today and makes you think about computers and technology taking over and making us obsolete. It just hit me in that right zeitgeist, at that right little point."

"As a poor kid, there was no YouTube or cell phones, and we didn’t have computers," reminisces Leguizamo. "I used to go to the Broadcast Museum on 53rd Street and listen to old radio plays like The Shadow and these incredible detective radio plays. It's a different art form. With no visuals, your voice has to create the world. I thought that was an incredible challenge."

DeBose portrays the Wild Blue Yonder's confident First Officer Becca Bang, who’s stranded in a temporal mystery that might engulf her at any moment.

"I've yet to do anything like this, which is always exciting," she adds. "I was a little afraid of it, which told me I should do it. These gents are so talented. It's great company. It’s coming from brilliant minds who build worlds so impeccably. And it's about real stuff we’re dealing with today. I liked the idea that we could tell this story and make people listen and create a space for them to imagine themselves in it and see how they problem-solve to make the world a bit better."

Recording your character's lines is far more difficult than just stepping up to the microphone to read a script, and our ace "Exoplanet" trio delivered in every session.

"I just go in and throw myself at something," DeBose recalls. "I don’t really have a method. But I can tell you this: it is a full-body workout. I use everything at my disposal, whether it’s a chair or a pillow or running in place. I just do what the character does and end up sweating. And after three days, I have to go sit in silence. It's great. I love the idea of bringing back the radio play. The art of listening is such a thing. I don’t think people listen enough. Even for myself."

Likewise, Leguizamo embraced his booth sessions with exuberance and authenticity.

"You have to cancel the rest of your day, especially if you've got to talk, because your vocal chords are fried," he admits. "And if they're not fried you're not doing your job. You really have to do the physicality. If you run you have to run like a maniac and be out of breath. If I'm getting hit I start punching myself. Then there’s a sexiness to doing these things. Your voice has to have a little mystery, a little naughtiness. It’s an interesting intersection where you pull it back a little and invite the audience in.”

For Mackie, who’s used to athleticism needed to play MCU superheroes like The Falcon and Captain America, recording dialogue offered a different type of opportunity.

“I look for reasons not to talk to people, so I love doing these," he jokes. "Once I’m out of the studio, I’m not talking to anybody for two days. At a certain point for me, I find mentally I get tired of hearing myself talk. I just have to come home at cut off. I’ve said enough words for the next month.”

"Exoplanet" is available now exclusively from Audible.

One crew. One planet. Three timelines. No one survives alone. A sci-fi thriller executive produced by Andy Weir (#1 bestselling author of Project Hail Mary, The Martian), with an all-star cast led by Anthony Mackie, Ariana DeBose and John Leguizamo.

Exoplanet is free for Audible members, but also available for purchase separately.

If you're thinking of picking up any big-ticket items soon—whether for yourself or as a gift for someone on your list— I would advise you to wait until you see what retailers have in store for the holiday sales season, which will kick off in earnest in mid-November. Walmart is the first major retailer to announce its Black Friday and Cyber Monday plan for 2026, and they actually involve three major shopping events between now and the December holidays.

As it did in 2025, Walmart will hold three sales between Nov. 12 and Dec. 2, as outlined in a recent press release. The sales will be both online and in-store, and will target a mix of Walmart+ members and general shoppers.

The events will take place on three different dates:

Walmart's Black Friday Deals Event 1: Nov. 12–15

Available online and in stores starting at 6 a.m. local time

Walmart+ members get six hours of early access beginning online at 12:01 a.m. ET on November 12

Walmart's Black Friday Deals Event 2: Nov. 23–29

Online only: Nov. 23 starting at 12:01 a.m. ET

Online and in stores: Nov. 27 starting at 6 a.m. local time

Walmart+ members get six hours of early access online beginning Nov. 23 at 12:01 a.m. ET

Walmart's Cyber Monday Deals Event: Nov. 30-Dec. 2

Walmart's physical stores will open at 6:00 a.m. local time on November 27 for their Black Friday events. Of course, they will start online earlier, so make sure to check their website as well.

Walmart says that you can expect discounts on electronics, toys, fashion, beauty, home goods, gifts, and everyday essentials. Although the company doesn't provide much in the way of specifics in its press release, last year's sales included discounts on TCL TVs, Dyson vacuums, Dreo appliances, and toys from LEGO.

The holiday season will see major sales from the same list of retailers that compete for your dollars throughout the year—namely, Best Buy, Target, and Amazon. Each will have its own version of Black Friday and Cyber Monday sales. So far, only Walmart has revealed its plans.

In short, yes. Though they aren't quite as impactful as they once were, Black Friday and Cyber Monday sales offer discounts that can be rare throughout the rest of the year. Whether it's a major purchase you've been saving up for or you’re shopping for gifts, it’s a good time to look for discounts, especially on items like appliances and electronics. Last year's sales included categories such as tech, home improvement, and fitness.

Black Friday used to be when major retailers offered their best deals on expensive tech and appliances, while Cyber Monday was for cheaper tech and gave smaller businesses a chance to compete online. Now, however, the distinction is almost meaningless: Every major retailer offers sales on both days, which means the smart move is to know what you want, use price trackers to monitor the deals, and refer to our Black Friday coverage, where we do all the searching, curating, and price tracking for you.

Strategy Adds 1,036 BTC While Repurchasing $188M of STRC Preferred Shares

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

Strategy’s newest capital-allocation update contains two transactions that point in different directions. In a November 9 filing announcement, the company said it acquired 1,036 additional Bitcoin and bought back approximately $188 million of STRC preferred shares. The first adds to exposure to BTC; the second reduces outstanding securities in its wider financing structure.

TL;DR: Strategy has disclosed a 1,036-BTC purchase alongside $188 million of STRC repurchases. The combination shows that the company’s Bitcoin strategy involves ongoing capital management, not simply accumulation. These are separate transactions with different effects on liquidity, risk and future obligations.

Strategy is closely associated with long-term Bitcoin accumulation, and for good reason: its treasury is far larger than that of most public companies. Yet the recurring filings increasingly include details about preferred securities, reserves and capital raising that are central to understanding how that position is financed.

A Bitcoin purchase turns available funds into an asset whose market value can change around the clock. Repurchasing preferred shares does something else. It reduces the quantity of a security issued to outside investors and may alter future distribution obligations, depending on that security’s terms. One action raises direct BTC exposure; the other changes the claims that investors have on Strategy’s capital structure.

That distinction was visible in an earlier update covered by NewsBTC, when Strategy bought 811 BTC and repurchased $208.2 million of STRC. The new announcement continues that paired approach, rather than revealing a sudden switch away from its central investment thesis.

Preferred securities can offer investors contractual distributions or other rights that differ from ordinary equity. Their terms, market prices and the company’s reserve position matter for both existing holders and common shareholders. Buying them back may be attractive when they trade at prices the issuer considers advantageous, but it also uses liquidity that could otherwise remain in reserve or fund more purchases.

There is a useful comparison with other Bitcoin treasury companies, which have their own funding methods and varying tolerance for leverage. The phrase ‘corporate Bitcoin buyer’ hides important differences in debt maturities, preferred distributions, equity issuance and the sources of cash used to acquire coins.

Strategy’s public metrics indicate Bitcoin holdings around 850,364 BTC following the update. Readers should use the company’s dated disclosure when comparing holdings and purchase prices rather than combining a fresh transaction with an older snapshot. Market-value figures can change between the filing and the moment a reader opens the article.

Another consideration is the difference between a repurchase authorization and completed repurchases. Here the company is describing actual STRC repurchase activity, which should be counted separately from any standing authority to buy securities later. The price paid and reduction in outstanding shares determine its economic impact; simply repeating the dollar amount cannot answer those questions.

None of this proves that the company’s securities or Bitcoin will rise. A more useful test is whether Strategy can keep servicing its obligations while making purchases in a period of changing interest rates and volatile digital-asset prices. The November 9 announcement confirms it is still doing both jobs—adding coins and adjusting the financing architecture that supports the strategy.

How ‘key person risk’ derails companies—and poses problems for Nvidia amid Jensen Huang scandal

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

When companies put all their faith and trust in one powerful leader, backup plans when things go south are hard to come by. Just ask Nvidia. The world’s second most valuable company (but as of today, the third) is currently in the throes of a major PR scandal that hasn’t just shaken trust in the tech company’s leader, but in the entire company.

Over the weekend, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang admitted to having an affair with a suspected Chinese spy. Amid the controversy, the company announced it would launch an independent investigation of the CEO’s relationship with Alice Huang, an executive from the Singapore-based tech company Megaspeed, over reports that he was the “target of a foreign intelligence effort.” 

As the company grapples with the controversy, and potential legal ramifications, concerns over the leader’s judgment loom large, especially given he’s been one of AI’s most vocal advocates and as China and the U.S. continue to battle it out to keep pace with one another in the AI race. While the company’s stock was trending down ahead of the midterm election, since the revelation, it’s been plummeting ever since. It now sits 22% below its October peak.

Turns out, there’s a phenomenon that explains the dangers of putting all your eggs in the basket of a single, all-powerful and important leader.

What is Key Man (or Person) Risk?

The ordeal is a prime example of something called key man (person) risk, which refers to when a company leans too heavily on one leader, making that leader’s own actions—their successes or failures—crucial for the entire company’s survival. That seems to be precisely the case for Nvidia, where Huang (a tech mastermind) has become, not just an asset, but utterly irreplaceable. That’s especially true because there is no clear successor for the powerful CEO.

As an analyst at Bernstein explained to Reuters, “Nvidia is not an ordinary company, and Jensen is not an ordinary CEO; he is Nvidia” and “clearly sets the long-term strategy and direction.”

Of course, all businesses have leaders—or maybe a few. But that doesn’t mean that companies should depend entirely on them for their most important connections, tasks, and undertakings all at once. According to Wright People PR, companies should seek to mitigate key person risk by cross-training employees, and focus on succession planning and team collaboration. 

Why the risk is growing

Still, as a handful of tech leaders become more and more powerful, key man risk seems to be accelerating. 

Dan Ives, partner and senior managing director of Yorkville Ives & Co., told CNN that it “has never been more of a blessing and a curse” than it is right now. 

“We’ve always had powerful CEOs but never in history have we been at a point where such an important industry is run by people you can count on two hands,” Ives explained.

In other words, it’s not just Huang and Nvidia who are exemplifying how to put all of your eggs in one basket. All of the largest tech companies and their single leaders—Meta’s Zuckerberg, Anthropic’s Amodei, Tesla and Space X’s Musk, and so on—are guilty.  That could mean that some of the most powerful companies are the most at risk. 

The phenomenon can also show up in politics, as it recently has come on full display following last week’s blue tsunami in the U.S. midterms: GOP leaders are increasingly backing away from President Donald Trump, as the Democrats’ blowout win highlights just how disillusioned they are with the policies of a Republican party that’s been remade in his image over the last decade.

As for Nvidia, the company is busy trying to downplay the controversy while surveying the damage. In a Nov. 8 press release, the company said, “The Board prioritizes US national and economic security, which is paramount to the interests of Nvidia and its shareholders,” it said in a statement. 

It noted that the board “has no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised.”

My Fall Outfits Were Feeling Stale—12 November Outfit Ideas I’m Trying Instead

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Now that the weather is cooling off, leaving the house involves grappling with the internal struggle: will I be cute or will I be comfortable today? On chilly days, the heaviest coats or most weather-proof shoes are all-too-appealing, but they don’t always show off your style in the way you might hope. Luckily, this month there are some clever ways you can meet in the sartorial middle: ensuring you don’t freeze, but still putting your outfit first.

With darker evenings here, football season in full swing, and Thanksgiving around the corner, think of these 12 November outfits as the puzzle pieces to help you check the boxes for everything coming up on your calendar. Playing with bold colors, contrasting textures, and even sneakily comfy silhouettes are some of the simple ways you can make November your best-dressed month of the entire year. Check out some of my favorite looks below.

An on-trend striped rugby sweater is the kind of dress-up-or-down staple you might usually style with jeans and sneakers. But for any dressy events on the calendar, try playing with texture and structure by styling it with a full, sequin skirt. It’s a look that reads dressy without trying too hard.

One way to make comfy pieces like oversized knits and track pants feel instantly dressy? By going bold with color. For days when you want to look polished but will mostly be relaxing on the couch, this is a great place to start.

It’s prime football season, which might mean your Sunday afternoon is devoted to keeping up with your favorite team. A pair of relaxed jeans and a suede jacket is a combination that’s cute enough to wear to brunch with your girlfriends, but won’t feel out of place at a sporty party.

If you’re the type to dress up for Thanksgiving (or any occasion really), a trim satin suit is a dressy look in stark contrast to the usual oversized staples. The shiny fabric gives the look a festive touch, while styled back to a simple black top and shoes, the overall effect is very ‘90s Prada—who can complain about that?

Odds are you’ll be headed out of town to spend some time with family. Your travel day look should always put comfort first, but a few small styling choices can make the outfit feel purposeful instead of schlubby. A crew neck tee paired with a V-neck sweater, a trench coat, and chunky Dad sneakers are what make Zoë Kravitz’ outfit look so good.

As winter approaches, there’s something enduringly chic about a white-on-white look. Even if your outfit is as simple as jeans and an oversized sweater, the matchiness pulls everything together. Black accessories are an easy pick, but you could also add a pop of contrast by styling the outfit with red shoes.

Call it genius, call it sneaky; there’s no need to wear full-on sweats to the Thanksgiving table. A dressy matching set can be just as comfortable. Look for styles with a soft, stretchy plissé fabric, which has a dressed-up, flowy air but feels as easy as your coziest loungewear.

This time of year, fashion is all about rich, sumptuous textures and hues. Gigi Hadid’s party look puts a soft, check flannel skirt in contrast with leather boots and a matching jacket, and the effect is greater than the sum of its parts. Cozy yet chic.

When you don’t want to think too hard about your look, an all-black outfit is an easy fallback with polish. Try opting for an oversized knit, styled with tailored trousers and loafers for a look you could wear to the office, but would feel just as comfortable in for a Saturday spent shopping with your most fashionable friends.

Breaking up a tailored look with a cummerbund or even a scarf can be an easy way to repurpose pieces you might usually relegate to the office. This dressy yet comfortable look is versatile, so throw it on for post-work martinis or even a night at the orchestra.

Fringe is a trend to know this season, and Nara Smith’s Kallmeyer dress is the blueprint for a festive Thanksgiving party or wedding when you want to dress to impress. The fringe detail adds movement, which makes it ideal for a situation with a dance floor. Layer a leather jacket or a black trench coat on top for your time in transit.

A great pair of jeans should always be in heavy rotation, but how you style them is an easy way to make them feel fresh. Here, mixing feminine pieces with more classic menswear details gives this a casual-yet-effortful effect. Try a blazer and little heels with socks for a playful spin on pieces you likely already have sitting in your closet.

She also writes a newsletter called Taeste Bud, that covers stuff we buy, places we go, and the people we want to be. Previously, she held roles at The Zoe Report, WhoWhatWear and Popsuagar. Democrats oppose plan to stack FCC with Republicans, plan probes of Chairman Carr

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Democrats accuse FCC nominee of evading questions about Carr, her current boss.

Senate Democrats today opposed President Trump’s nominee for an open Federal Communications Commission seat, and criticized Republicans for moving to confirm a new commissioner during the period before Democrats take control of Congress in January 2027. Democrats also plan to use their majority next year to launch probes of FCC Chairman Brendan Carr.

“After the American people last week overwhelmingly rejected the MAGA agenda, there is no justification for this committee to advance partisan nominees during the lame-duck period,” Sen. Ed Markey (D-Mass.) said today at the Commerce Committee’s nomination hearing for Danielle Thumann Severs, Trump’s pick to fill an empty commissioner’s seat.

Thumann Severs, an attorney and senior counsel to Carr, faced questions from Democrats about whether she supports Carr’s attacks on TV broadcasters.

“Do you support Chairman Carr’s illegal campaign to remove Jimmy Kimmel from the nation’s airwaves?” Sen. Ben Ray Luján (D-N.M.) asked the nominee.

Thumann Severs responded that she has not advised Carr on media-related topics and declined Luján’s request to identify any specific policy issues on which she disagrees with Carr.

Luján predicted trouble for Carr when Democrats take the majority in both chambers of Congress. He seemed to suggest that Carr will quickly grow tired of having to testify in Congress multiple times a month under the Democratic majorities.

“I don’t think Chairman Carr is going to be around more than another three or four months because when we have the gavels in here, he will be here once a month,” Luján said. “And Mr. Carr, if you’re listening now, know you’re going to be in this body once a month, if not more, and in the House of Representatives as well, answering questions, and you don’t have the protection of the gavel any longer.” Luján said Democrats will use “subpoena authorities that you have to respond to so we can get to the bottom of the nonsense and the cover-ups that have been taking place.”

Markey accused Thumann Severs of evading questions about Carr threatening and taking actions against broadcasters on behalf of Trump. “Anyone seeking a seat on the Federal Communications Commission must be willing to stand up to such threats and to defend the First Amendment, and you’re not willing to do so here in your confirmation hearing,” Markey said.

Democrats similarly criticized Thumann Severs for not giving a clear answer on whether the FCC has authority to eliminate E-Rate, a $2 billion-a-year program that was created by Congress to connect students and library visitors to the Internet. Carr has proposed eliminating or scaling back the program. Thumann Severs said she helped Carr work on the E-Rate Notice of Proposed Rulemaking, which seeks public comment about the program and the FCC’s authority to change or eliminate it.

“I can’t prejudge the open proceeding [on E-Rate] at the FCC right now,” she said today.

Sen. Tammy Duckworth (D-Ill.) criticized Thumann Severs’ answers. “If you’re confirmed, you’re going to be one of the votes that decides whether the FCC stays an independent regulator, or becomes an arm of the White House press office. We deserve to know where you stand, and I am not comfortable with your answers today,” Duckworth said.

Duckworth told Thumann Severs that the FCC cannot eliminate E-Rate without Congressional approval. “We never directed the FCC to eliminate the program and frankly, at a time when the price of gas, groceries, and utilities is skyrocketing, the last thing the FCC should be doing is making it harder for schools to stay connected,” Duckworth said.

The Senate Commerce, Science & Transportation Committee held today’s hearing for Thumann Severs and three other nominees for federal positions. Senators have until Thursday to submit additional questions for the record, and nominees will have until November 16 to respond.

Thumann Severs needs approval from the committee and then the full Senate to be confirmed to the FCC. Committee Chairman Ted Cruz (R-Texas) is trying to get her approved before Republicans have to give up their Senate majority.

Thumann Severs would give Republicans an unusual 3-1 advantage on the FCC, which usually operates with a 3-2 split. Democrats criticized Republicans for not pairing her nomination with a Democratic one. The commission has only one Democrat, Anna Gomez, and she would have to leave at the end of 2027 if she is not given another term.

“For many decades, the Senate has paired commission nominees: A Republican and a Democrat moving together,” Sen. John Hickenlooper (D-Colo.) said. “Congress established the FCC as an independent, bipartisan agency… a bipartisan FCC is essential to avoid things like large-scale media consolidation, and I think also more fundamentally to protect a free and diverse press.”

Because US law requires the FCC to have three commissioners for a quorum, Gomez’s “presence in the current 2-1 Republican majority is necessary for the FCC to conduct business,” Hickenlooper said. The Communications Act prohibits a single party from holding more FCC seats than is necessary to form a majority, but Hickenlooper predicted that Trump will fire Gomez to give Republicans a 3-0 stranglehold.

“If today’s nominee is confirmed, President Trump could then fire Commissioner Gomez and have an FCC with no Democratic voice at all,” Hickenlooper said.

Hickenlooper questioned whether Thumann Severs is sufficiently independent from Carr. “Ms. Thumann Severs, your career has largely been spent serving Brendan Carr,” Hickenlooper said. “[You are] currently Chairman Carr’s senior counsel; previously, you were legal advisor to Commissioner Carr. Your official duties include near-daily meetings with Chairman Carr and working to advance his agenda, amplify his messaging.”

Markey asked Thumann Severs if it was appropriate for Carr to use the FCC’s regulatory authority to pressure broadcasters into taking a comedian off the air. Markey was referring to an incident last year in which Carr threatened to revoke ABC broadcast TV licenses over remarks made by Kimmel, who was then suspended by ABC owner Disney.

“We can do this the easy way or the hard way,” Carr said at the time, adding that Disney must “take action” on Kimmel “or there’s going to be additional work for the FCC ahead.”

When asked about Carr’s comments today, Thumann Severs said, “Those were his words. They were not my words, and I have not advised him or worked with the chairman on these particular issues. I can say that if I were to be confirmed as commissioner, I would make all of my decisions [based on] the facts and the law and the record.”

Senate Democrats tried to get Thumann Severs to discuss Carr’s decision to order an early review of ABC station licenses. “Do you think the FCC should revoke broadcasters’ licenses because the president does not like their coverage?” Duckworth asked.

Thumann Severs answered that the FCC “has a responsibility to fully investigate any complaint… and to evaluate that complaint on the merits. I don’t think a decision should be made purely based on someone’s discontent with the content being aired.”

Although the ABC license review is widely seen as retaliation against the network for its news coverage and other programming, Carr’s proceeding is ostensibly based on an allegation that Disney’s diversity, equity, and inclusion (DEI) practices are discriminatory.

“Based on publicly available material, which is all I have seen on that particular matter, the licenses were called for early renewal as part of an investigation into potential rule violations,” Thumann Severs said today.

Cruz mentioned the Carr FCC’s review of ABC licenses and noted that “ABC argues [the review] is actually in retaliation for the network’s programming.” Cruz also alleged that in Democratic administrations, “the FCC has previously used its public interest standard as a weapon against conservatives.”

“If you are confirmed, will you commit to basing licensing decisions on the law and the record and to not using the FCC’s authority to pressure or punish a broadcaster for protected speech?” Cruz asked the nominee. She answered, “Yes.”

Cruz then asked Thumann Severs if she supports the JAWBONE Act, a bill that Cruz introduced with Sen. Ron Wyden (D-Ore.) to enable lawsuits against federal officials who try to coerce broadcasters or tech platforms into restricting speech.

Thumann Severs answered that she supports the bill. “I think it’s incredibly important that federal officials not use their position… to coerce against protected speech,” she said.

Cruz seemed satisfied with her answers. While Democrats recognized that Republicans have the votes to advance the nomination, they spent much of the hearing criticizing Thumann Severs’ answers and the Republican plan to confirm her without a paired Democratic nominee.

“We should not be confirming a Republican nominee without also renominating and confirming Anna Gomez, the Democrat, and filling the other vacant Democratic seat,” Markey said. “There is a potential next year where there will be three Republicans and no Democrats on the Federal Communications Commission… that is wrong, and that is why I do not believe we should proceed with this nomination at this time.”

After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.
