Microsoft Fixes Nearly 1,100 Security Vulnerabilities

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Microsoft did another four-digit patch drop today, releasing updates to fix almost 1,100 security vulnerabilities in its Windows operating systems and supported software. This month’s Patch Tuesday addresses 63 critical flaws — including a zero-day in Microsoft Outlook — and comes amid important security updates from other software makers, including Adobe, Apple, Google and Mozilla.

Image: Shutterstock.com. Diorama by @zepp1969.

November’s patch release from Redmond tackles 1,093 vulnerabilities, fixing 63 “critical” flaws that can be exploited to seize control over a Windows system with little or no help from users. Nearly half of those critical bugs are remote code execution vulnerabilities. Overall, 535 of the flaws addressed this month allow an attacker to elevate their privileges on a vulnerable device.

This latest round of updates means Microsoft has shipped more than 5,100 security patches so far this year, a staggering number considering that a year ago Microsoft was averaging around 100 patches per month. Microsoft credits advances in artificial intelligence for helping to find (and fix) such a large volume of security bugs in recent months. Last month, Microsoft pushed more than 1,400 fixes.

According to Redmond, just one of the flaws is already seeing active exploitation: CVE-2026-124194, a spoofing bug in Outlook. The advisory for CVE-2026-124194 says it affects Microsoft 365 Apps for Enterprise and editions of Microsoft Office.

Several other major software makers pushed security updates this week to fix critical weaknesses in their products. Apple on Monday released out-of-cycle security updates to fix at least nine vulnerabilities in iOS/iPadOS, macOS, watchOS and visionOS. Among the weaknesses reportedly fixed in Apple’s release include an iMessage flaw that could let a remote attacker join a group chat and receive its messages; the updates also fix a separate iMessage flaw that could let someone added to a group access messages sent before they joined.

Lee Neely, an instructor at the SANS Institute, noted that Apple backported the fix to iOS 18.

“I take notice when Apple releases fixes to more than two versions,” Neely wrote in a SANS newsletter today. “You really need to get to version 27.2.1 of their OSes and push folks who are holding onto devices which cannot run the current version. The best plan often involves setting an upgrade date for old hardware. Run through all your Apple devices and make sure everything is up to date, don’t assume the fixes went out automatically.”

Google this week released Chrome version 157.0.8103.50, which resolves nine security holes, including CVE-2026-125350, the sixth zero-day vulnerability this year for Chrome (this bug also being patched in Chromium-based browsers like Edge). The latest Chrome version follows its November 3 release, which fixed 213 vulnerabilities. A new version of Mozilla Firefox out today (Firefox 160) resolves almost 100 CVEs, about half of them high severity.

Adobe today released patches for at least 110 vulnerabilities across a range of products, with nearly half of the flaws rated high severity. Adobe said there is no evidence any of the security holes it addressed this month are being actively exploited.

Chris Goettl, vice president of product management at Ivanti, said it’s easy for security teams at large enterprises to feel overwhelmed by the volume and criticality of patches from Microsoft lately. But he notes that while some 95,000 vulnerabilities have been assigned CVEs this year across all vendors, only 217 have so far this year been added to the Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) list maintained by the U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA).

“Roughly four in ten of this year’s exploited vulnerabilities sit in operating systems, endpoint software, browsers, and server platforms, which a continuous patch strategy can close quickly,” Goettl said.”The rest calls for exposure management alongside patching, especially the 44% that sit in edge devices and IT management or remote access tools, the internet-facing systems that hold privileged access. Teams that can quickly identify those assets, confirm exposure and act on confirmed exploitation can focus on the small slice of vulnerabilities attackers are actively using, while the rest stays on the normal patch cadence.”

Please don’t neglect to back up any important data if you haven’t recently; generally speaking, this is a task best accomplished before applying operating system updates. As ever, if you experience any issues applying this month’s bundle of love from Microsoft, please drop a comment about it in the space below.

The Subpoenas Are Coming, the Subpoenas Are Coming: Tech Execs Brace for Congressional Crackdown, Say Lobbyists

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On Thursday, I was on the phone with a source when he suddenly sounded like he’d gotten the wind knocked out of him. We were in the middle of talking about antitrust policy when he exhaled and said, “Wow. Worst one-day destruction of value in history. Just got the Wall Street Journal alert.”

The tech world—and the markets built on top of it—is still figuring out how to feel about last week’s momentous Democratic midterm sweep. My Republican sources, unsurprisingly, reported that the vibes in their ranks are “horrendous.” Anyone who works with data centers is just about ready to throw in the towel on planet Earth and take their infrastructure efforts to space, with voters having opposed data center construction via 40 different ballot measures. And then there’s the more general rebuke of the future the tech industry is building, and the possible implications for an economy that’s increasingly come to revolve around it.

Indeed, it seems that the debate over AI has really, truly broken through to the mainstream this year: According to an NBC exit poll, 56% of voters called AI and data centers a “crucial or major factor” in their votes for Congress. Amid the free-for-all of President Trump’s first two years back in office, AI companies have been allowed to develop their technology largely free of regulatory oversight, but they may now be forced to pump the brakes.

In a lengthy interview with Ross Douthat on CBS’s 60 Minutes, Trump said he was open to working with Democrats to implement some AI guardrails. Still, no one I spoke to this week told me they expect to see sweeping AI legislation—unless, as two different tech Democrats suggested, there is an AI-caused catastrophe that “scares the shit out of us.”

Democratic Senators like Bernie Sanders and Elizabeth Warren have already begun making plans to investigate tech corporations.

In the absence of such an event, “we expect [new AI laws] to be narrow and bipartisan. Child safety, AI agent liability, and predeployment testing, for example, all have Republican cosponsors,” said Christina Ho, an executive at the financial auditing firm Oath Verified.

Meanwhile, even more explicitly MAGA-coded industries could see more concrete crackdowns. Those in “the crypto world,” who spent the first year of Trump 2.0 flying high, now “realize they’re screwed,” said an executive at a conservative think tank—especially as famed crypto critic Sherrod Brown makes his big return to the Senate with, as one Democratic strategist put it, an “axe to grind.”

Defense-tech founders and investors may also find themselves toning down their rhetoric as the testosterone-fueled “Department of War” era comes to an end with the possible impeachment of Secretary Pete Hegseth. In recent years, “dual use” has become a bit of a “dirty word,” said Cody Huggins, a partner at Scout Ventures. He suspects that promoting the commercial applications of military technologies, from satellites to drones, “will come back to be a little bit more sexy than it has been.”

Perhaps the biggest winners of the post-midterms period will be the DC law firms brought on to help manage the flood of subpoenas promised by politicians like Bernie Sanders and Elizabeth Warren. “AI executives might be called to testify,” said Ho, adding, “Companies would be wise to prepare their executives for the witness table before they prepare for any new laws.”

A Democratic tech lobbyist told me that one of his clients has already received a list of questions from a congressional committee. “They’re not waiting to have gavels,” he said.

Luther Lowe, the head of public policy at the start-up accelerator Y Combinator, is hoping to see a return to the antitrust revolution once promised by former FTC chair Lina Khan. “I think some of that stuff could come back online,” he said, referring to antitrust legislation that then Senate majority leader Chuck Schumer declined to bring to a vote in 2022.

In the future, tech executives who went all in on MAGA (right after going all in on woke) may want to consider some age-old advice: just be yourself. “Companies need to stop thinking about political outcomes as permanent conditions. Regulations change, governments change, and priorities change. That’s not new,” said Andrew Peek, CEO of the investing platform Tilt. “What’s different now is the sheer amount of money being committed to technologies whose economics are still evolving. If your entire strategy only works under one political scenario, that’s a pretty big risk to be taking.”

Still, there was one midterm fight in which the tech world definitively won out: the one over Proposition 40, or California’s so-called billionaire tax, which was defeated. Some suspect that the hundreds of millions of dollars spent by Silicon Valley billionaires to defeat the measure could backfire. “There’s a growing wave of anger and resentment from the working class that is coming for them,” said the Democratic strategist. The irony of spending so much money to fight the tax, he said, is that if it had passed, it could have “temporarily removed some of the pressure in the pressure cooker.” All that money having been spent to shut down the tax, he warned, “is gonna make things much worse.”

Part one of Side Effects Guaranteed launches in December

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Outer Worlds 2 is finally getting its DLC. Obsidian has announced that Side Effects Guaranteed, the first of a "two-part expansion" series, will launch on December 2. The devs had been promising DLC since before launch as part of the RPG's $100 Premium Edition, but you'd be forgiven for worrying that those promises were forgotten amid the massive shakeup at the studio.

The first part of Side Effects Guaranteed will offer a new region to explore on Eden, a boosted level cap with new perks grab, four new flaws to spice up your build, new gear, and an additional radio station. The story will have you working with one of two Sub Rosa agents as you decide what to do with the hibernating crew of a derelict ship.

We spoke with Obsidian about The Outer Worlds 2 DLC shortly after the game's launch last year, and at that time they'd only begun to sketch out plans for the expansion. But at least one thing has carried over from those high-level ideas: "Maybe we'll lean a bit harder into some of the more minor factions, like Sub Rosa, and build them out a little bit," game director Brandon Adler told us in 2025, and that part of the plan seems to have borne out.

But a lot has happened at Obsidian since we had that chat. It's now under Bethesda's direct management and working on a new Fallout game as part of Xbox's big shakeup. Obsidian was not immune to the mass layoffs at the publisher, and seemingly had an Avowed sequel stripped away as Xbox pointed it to return to the more lucrative Fallout franchise.

While The Outer Worlds 2 was one of our favorite games of 2025, it never appeared to make much of a splash among the broader gaming community. Its fans feared that slow sales might lead to the DLC being canceled, and there's still some fear that the expansions have been truncated so Obsidian can move onto Fallout more quickly.

On this day in space! Nov. 10, 2013: Europe's GOCE satellite, the 'Ferrari of space,' fell out of orbit after mapping Earth's gravity field

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Controllers weren't sure where GOCE would fall back to Earth. Some people worried that it might crash into a populated area.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

On Nov. 10, 2013, Europe's GOCE satellite fell to Earth after four years in orbit mapping the planet's gravitational field.

GOCE, its name was short for Gravity field and steady-state Ocean Circulation Explorer, launched in 2009 on a mission to map Earth's gravity like never before. But despite that successful mission, it was doomed in 2013 when it ran out of fuel.

At the time, controllers weren't sure where GOCE would fall back to Earth. Some people worried that it might crash into a populated area. However, the small spacecraft broke up in the atmosphere and didn't cause any property damage.

It disintegrated about 50 miles above the Falkland Islands in the South Atlantic Ocean. The European Space Agency called the mission a success. GOCE lasted far longer than expected. It showed small variations in Earth's gravity, mapped the structure of Earth's crust and mantle, and tracked ocean currents.

The GOCE satellite was a sleek spacecraft, with fins covered in solar panels that gave it a futuristic design. In fact, its look earned it a pretty cool nickname.

"GOCE's sleek, elegant aerodynamic design — responsible for it being dubbed the 'Ferrari of space' — immediately set it apart from most other satellites,." ESA officials said in a description.

There was more than looks behind GOCE's design. In order to generate the best map ever of Earth's gravitational field, GOCE had to fly in an orbit so low — just 139 miles (224 kilometers) — it needed to counteract the drag from the gossamer thin atmosphere of Earth at that altitude. The International Space Station orbits Earth at nearly twice that altitude.

GOCE was powered by an xenon ion engine that allowed to maintain its low orbit for four years. Once it ran out of fuel, the 1-ton satellite could not maintain its position in space, leading to a fiery plunge to Earth.

The large size of GOCE — it weighed 2,425 pounds (1,100 kilograms) and was 17 feet (5.3 meters) long and 3.2 feet (1 m) wide — made its reentry stand out, particularly since at least 25% of the satellite was expected to reach the Earth's surface.

Want more space history? Check out our full On This Day In Space Story archive and watch our On This Day In Space videos on YouTube.

On this day in space! Nov. 9, 1967: NASA launches the Saturn V moon rocket for the first time on Apollo 4, but no astronauts were aboard

Hanneke Weitering is a multimedia journalist in the Pacific Northwest reporting on the future of aviation at FutureFlight.aero and Aviation International News and was previously the Editor for Spaceflight and Astronomy news here at Space.com. As an editor with over 10 years of experience in science journalism she has previously written for Scholastic Classroom Magazines, MedPage Today and The Joint Institute for Computational Sciences at Oak Ridge National Laboratory. After studying physics at the University of Tennessee in her hometown of Knoxville, she earned her graduate degree in Science, Health and Environmental Reporting (SHERP) from New York University. Hanneke joined the Space.com team in 2016 as a staff writer and producer, covering topics including spaceflight and astronomy. She currently lives in Seattle, home of the Space Needle, with her cat and two snakes. In her spare time, Hanneke enjoys exploring the Rocky Mountains, basking in nature and looking for dark skies to gaze at the cosmos.

Forward secured a $50 million credit line and committed up to $50 million to a tokenized U.S. home-price product.

Forward Industries added roughly 226,000 SOL to its treasury through purchases and staking rewards since the end of September.

The company said Tuesday that its holdings reached approximately 8.7 million SOL, worth roughly $726.5 million, totaling about 1.5% of Solana's total circulating supply.

Forward controls the largest Solana treasury among publicly traded companies by a wide margin, with its closest rival, DeFi Development Corp., holding just over 2.6 million SOL.

The update comes amid a slide in SOL and Forward's own shares Forward Industries, Inc. (FWDI). SOL dropped as low as $82 on Tuesday, its lowest level in almost three months, before recovering to around $83.50. Forward shares also dipped to a near three-month low of $5.25, leaving the stock down more than 28% so far this year.

Forward also disclosed that it had secured a two-year, $50 million credit facility from an unnamed U.S. national bank. The loan is backed by SOL that will remain staked, which will allow Forward to continue earning rewards.

The company added that it committed up to $50 million to USHP, a tokenized product that tracks U.S. home prices whose launch was also anchored by the Galaxy-Sharplink Onchain Yield Fund. It plans to use income from the investment to purchase more SOL.

Barbara Tfank Spring 2027 Ready-to-Wear

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In the age of AI, Barbara Tfank is, refreshingly, all about craft. Unlike other designers who may have moved on, she’s stayed true to her passion for eveningwear. As such, she continues to seek out fabrics from European textile mills she’s used since day one. This season, she looked to her vast archive and found a few unused Bianchini Férier prints to repurpose for her spring collection. “The artist Raoul Dufy actually painted and designed the textiles for them, so they’re collectibles,” she explained of the lively, lush florals in vivid hues. “I was just so happy to see these in my archive because there’s nothing digital about them, it was all done by hand and just feels timeless.”

With the prints, she revamped some beloved Tfank styles like her signature off-the-shoulder gowns in new spring-ready organza. In addition to her retrained column gowns, she cut one dress a touch shorter than she tends to lean toward—now reaching to mid-shin—in a bid to cater to the emerging casual evening gown customer (think someone who might want to wear a pair of thong heels rather than a proper Manolo when going out).

Along those same lines, she explored a few casual styles, like the bra tops that have been everywhere on spring 2027 runways, hers made in a groovy Férier print and paired with wide-leg pants. Other options include an airy halterneck dress with a long train and a casual navy-and-white sundress, for which Tfank mentions the fabric was once used to create a custom coat for First Lady Michelle Obama. Talk about divine timing to bring the print back.

The earnings announcement lands at a particularly volatile moment for CoreWeave and its neocloud peers, as the market sours on the AI trade and as Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, the central figure in the global buildout, faces a scandal tied to an affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

Here's how the company performed relative to LSEG consensus:

CoreWeave's revenue soared 168% from about $1.36 billion a year earlier, according to a statement. The company recorded a net loss of $709 million, or $1.27 per share, compared to a net loss of $110 million a year earlier. Adjusted earnings exclude stock-based compensation.

Executives pushed up their forecast for 2026. They now see $1.275 billion to $1.375 billion in adjusted operating income on $13 billion to $13.3 billion in revenue. Analysts surveyed by Visible Alpha were looking for $1.07 billion in adjusted operating income, and analysts polled by LSEG had expected $12.86 billion in 2026 revenue.

In August, CoreWeave projected $960 million to $1.15 billion in adjusted operating income and $12.4 billion to $13.2 billion in revenue.

CoreWeave has lost over 40% of its value in the past month, as investors rotate out of companies most closely tied to AI infrastructure. Rising interest rates have pushed lenders out of the market, raising concerns about ongoing access to capital. Some CoreWeave bond yields recently topped 16%.

"The business can execute well and still face a tougher financing environment," Joe Tigay, portfolio manager at Equity Armor Investments, wrote in an email. "Strong demand is encouraging; the cost and availability of capital remain critical."

CoreWeave will get most the capital it needs for 2027 through clients pre-payments and financing from hardware partners, and the company will raise "a limited amount" as well, CEO Mike Intrator told analysts on a conference call.

The company plans to raise new debt tied to a set of contracts in the weeks to come, said Nitin Agrawal, its finance chief.

"At current yields, we do not intend to issue additional parent-level unsecured debt through 2027," he said. "By contrast, the asset-level secured debt market has remained stable, with our loans continuing to trade around par value."

Fellow neocloud Nebius, whose stock is down by a similar amount, is scheduled to report earnings on Thursday. Nebius shares got a small boost from CoreWeave's results.

Both companies and several other providers of cloud-based graphics processing units are particularly reliant on Nvidia, which provides GPUs as well as necessary capital to the whole ecosystem. Nvidia shares have dropped nearly 10% to start the week as the Huang affair raises pressure on the company in Washington, D.C. Nvidia's board said on Tuesday that it hired Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to investigate Huang and determine whether national security or company secrets were compromised.

CoreWeave investors are also looking toward next week, when key customer Anthropic is scheduled to debut on the Nasdaq. In its updated IPO prospectus on Tuesday, Anthropic indicated that it could be valued at around $1.4 trillion after its share sale.

During the quarter, CoreWeave said it would establish its first data centers in Asia and announced the Forge service for running and evaluating AI models.

Management still anticipates 1.85 gigawatts in active power by the end of 2026. CoreWeave is targeting $37 billion to $39 billion in full-year capital expenditures, which would imply 155% growth at the middle of the range. Analysts polled by Visible Alpha were looking for $37.69 billion.

CoreWeave owed almost $47 billion in debt at the end of the third quarter, according to the statement. Like its bigger cloud rivals such as Amazon and Oracle, CoreWeave is rushing to bolster computing capacity for clients. But last week, voters in the U.S., where CoreWeave derives most of its revenue, rejected dozens of initiatives on data center development.

"If data center construction across the sector were to slow, installed compute becomes scarcer, and the knock-on effect is that GPU pricing may stay higher for longer," said Intrator, a CoreWeave co-founder.

He said the company does not expect risks to deliveries in 2027 based on construction at permitted locations.

"We could sell all of the capacity that we have to current buyers that are in the queue. We are choosing not to," Intrator said. We're choosing to be patient. We're choosing to bring the infrastructure to market when it is further along, because we believe that that will result in better economic output for the outstanding infrastructure that we have."

Meanwhile, demand remains robust, with the company now sitting on a $132.5 billion revenue backlog, up from $104 billion as of June.

"Should we choose to focus only on our committed backlog, we would expect capex for 2027 to remain in line with our 2026 capex investment, while revenue and EBITDA grow significantly," Agrawal said.

Pas tendre Zoumana !

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Allez Paillade, le site non officiel dédié à l'actualité du Montpellier HSC revient sur la surprenante défaite à domicile des Héraultais contre le FC Metz (2-3) et relate les propos du coach Zoumana Camara, pas tendre avec ses joueurs qui ont laissé filer une occasion de rester dans la roue des Verts :

"Je suis déçu. La leçon, c’est qu’un match se joue 90 minutes. On a été absent des débats sur les 45 premières minutes. Et même sur cette deuxième mi-temps où on est revenu avec de meilleures intentions. Quand on met ce 2-1, on ne doit pas encaisser ce troisième but. On a le sentiment d’avoir fait le plus dur, de pouvoir emballer le match et de créer le doute. Et derrière, on prend un troisième but quelques minutes après. Le sentiment que rien ne tourne dans le bon sens dans cette soirée.

L’absence d’agressivité, dans l’intensité, dans le rythme. Quand on ne met pas ces ingrédients-là, on tombe face à une équipe où il y avait un tout. Une équipe qui a pris un sévère revers à la maison, qui vient de perdre dans la semaine son président historique. Et puis un ancien joueur de la maison qui revient. Donc vous mettez tout ça ensemble, ça crée un esprit et une équipe qui est venue en mode commando, en mode combat avec l’envie de faire quelque chose. Et si nous, on n’a pas la capacité à avoir au moins cette même envie, cette même agressivité, cette même détermination avant de parler du jeu, on ne peut pas exister dans ce championnat et on n’a pas existé aujourd’hui, en tout cas sur la première période.

(Un relâchement ?) Peut-être. J’ai des jeunes joueurs, on est en train d’apprendre un peu dans tout. C’est l’avenir qui dira si c’est un accident ou pas. Mais en tout cas, on était absent des débats aujourd’hui, surtout sur la première mi-temps.

(Un coup d’arrêt ?) Une saison, c’est long. C’est très très long. L’environnement était euphorique. Et parfois ultra-enthousiaste. J’ai dû la saison passée venir beaucoup de fois ici. On parlait d’être un peu plus réaliste face à la situation. Aujourd’hui, les résultats faisaient qu’il y avait cette euphorie. Et c’est juste à juste titre. C’est à nous de devoir, à l’intérieur, canaliser ça, garder les pieds sur terre, continuer à avancer et continuer à mettre les ingrédients qui ont fait la réussite. Et là, on ne les a pas mis ce soir."

Lumbra Raises $25M Series A to Expand Controlled AI Agent Deployments

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Lumbra announced on November 10, 2026 that it raised a $25 million Series A co-led by Point72 Ventures and Ansa, with continued participation from existing investor Costanoa, to expand its controlled AI-agent platform beyond national security into regulated commercial sectors.

Most of the proceeds will fund engineers and deployment teams pursuing customers in finance, healthcare, law and other regulated industries. Lumbra is also hiring across product and go-to-market roles, including new graduates. The company says its agents already operate in production on top-secret networks across the Pentagon and U.S. Intelligence Community.

LumbraOS is model-neutral, allowing customers to combine frontier-lab models with open-source alternatives. The platform has three components: Nebula manages agent execution, StarChart holds organizational knowledge, and Astrolabe handles evaluation. Nebula links individual claims to their sources, records what agents do and applies data protections. Domain experts must validate results before an agent can write them back to a system of record.

StarChart’s knowledge graph stores insights and conclusions alongside their sources and sensitivity labels. Customers retain the data rights and can export their graphs. Astrolabe evaluates agents ahead of rollout, then continues scoring live systems against custom benchmarks. Each agent accesses information where it is stored and under that source’s permissions, so customer data does not need to be duplicated.

Lumbra’s technology documentation describes evaluation as a continuous process, with every result traceable through its reasoning, underlying documents and the checks it passed. Validation compares outputs with reference material that people specify and maintain, instead of depending only on another model to judge the output. The orchestration around stochastic models is designed to be deterministic, giving oversight teams a repeatable execution path whenever the workflow, context and evaluation criteria are unchanged. The platform supports air-gapped, on-premises and sovereign-cloud deployments, with no data leaving the boundary, audited dependencies and signed, reproducible container images.

Co-founder and CEO Aaron Brown described the intended change in how operators supervise agents: “The organizations that get agents right will spend less time telling them what to do and more time deciding what they may touch and what their work must prove.”

Lumbra reports two early government milestones alongside the round: it signed its first U.S. intelligence-agency contract two months after product work began and moved a later Department of War contract from award to production on top-secret networks in six months.

The Department of War launched Agent Network on June 25, 2026, naming Palantir Technologies and Lumbra in its official release as contributors to a project led by the Chief Digital and Artificial Intelligence Office with U.S. Pacific, Southern and European Commands. The system uses agents to examine defense intelligence and operational systems, turning findings into options for commanders. Target selection and strikes remain human decisions; the system does not take either action autonomously. Testing, operational evaluation and oversight will continue during development and fielding.

Commercial work is already at the engagement stage, according to Lumbra’s domains documentation. Financial-data providers and research teams are working with the company on diligence, sanctions screening and supply-chain risk. Target workflows also include financial research and know-your-customer and anti-money-laundering checks. The clinical work includes active engagements and live surgical-preparation workflows, with revenue-cycle and population-health uses identified as adjacent applications.

The Series A therefore connects Lumbra’s commercial ambitions to a specific test: whether controls built for classified networks can meet the governance demands of regulated buyers while the company adds the engineering and deployment staff needed to support them.

IBM and Microsoft Launch Imagine Enterprise for Industry AI Agents

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

IBM Consulting and Microsoft announced Imagine Enterprise on November 10, 2026, a jointly developed platform for creating, operating and overseeing AI agents tailored to industry processes, drawing on IBM Consulting’s inventory of more than 6,000 pre-built AI agents and assets.

The platform pairs IBM’s industry and delivery expertise with Microsoft’s AI, data, cloud, identity and security technologies. It is designed to connect with an enterprise’s existing data, processes and systems, with the aim of letting organizations adapt pre-built capabilities without replacing their current technology environment or assembling each component themselves.

Forward-deployed engineers from both companies can work with customer teams on qualifying engagements to help move projects from experimentation into production. Clients can engage IBM Consulting to deploy and operate the platform, collaborate with IBM on enterprise-scale implementations, or choose self-management.

Initial industry frameworks cover financial services, insurance and retail. Manufacturing, energy, the public sector and mobility are identified only as possible future additions.

IBM’s launch overview says the companies jointly invested in and co-developed the platform. IBM Consulting Advantage’s Process Studio and Context Studio connect agents to an organization’s own data and processes, while Microsoft Foundry, GitHub Copilot and Copilot Studio help coordinate agents with enterprise systems. Microsoft Fabric, Microsoft 365, Entra, Purview, Defender and Azure provide the surrounding data, identity, governance, security and cloud foundation.

That product division places governance inside the deployment stack rather than leaving it to a separate oversight exercise. For regulated industries, the platform includes controls, observability and AI-compliance support intended to govern agents connected to enterprise knowledge and daily work.

IBM says every agent action, decision and escalation is logged, with real-time data on latency, cost, token usage and run history. Governance controls set which actions an agent may take autonomously, which require human approval and when escalation is mandatory, and agents cannot bypass their defined authority limits.

An operational intelligence layer monitors performance and recommends improvements, with approved changes redeployed automatically. Microsoft Defender, Purview, Entra and Sentinel add real-time visibility over agents in production. The operating model therefore leaves defined approval decisions with people while automating performance monitoring and the redeployment of approved changes.

IBM’s example applies the model to insurance claims, a document-intensive process that runs from first notification of loss through settlement. Based on client interviews, standard operating procedures and industry benchmarks, the process is divided among digital workers with defined responsibilities, controls and escalation paths.

A Digital Steward orchestrates the work. It assesses claim complexity, flags possible fraud signals, checks policy coverage and routes cases according to their characteristics and an adjuster’s authority. Simple claims can go to junior adjusters, coverage ambiguity to senior adjusters, and complex cases with multiple anomaly flags to principal adjusters or specialist investigation units.

The employee receives a prioritized queue explaining why work was routed, what has already been investigated and the recommended next action. The example leaves the claim decision with the human; digital workers handle preparation, triage and follow-through.

SEI, a provider of financial technology, operations and asset-management services, is exploring emerging and agentic technologies with IBM Consulting and Microsoft support. It is also using other technologies from both companies to improve its operations and the experiences it delivers to employees and clients. Its role in the announcement is exploratory, without quantified production results for Imagine Enterprise.

Sneha Shah, SEI’s Chief AI Strategist and Head of SEI Next, said: “Our work with IBM Consulting and Microsoft is helping us explore how emerging capabilities could support critical processes and be responsibly scaled over time, with the goal to create better experiences for our employees and clients.”

The launch connects a large consulting asset base to an extensive enterprise technology stack, but its adoption model depends on more than supplying agents. The initial industry frameworks, approval boundaries and deployment options define a specific execution test: turning pre-built components into governed operations that fit the way individual enterprises already work.

On-Device Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech in Google AI Edge Gallery

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Today, we are introducing on-device Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) and Text-to-Speech (TTS) capabilities in Google AI Edge Gallery. Voice is often the most natural way to interact with AI on phones and wearables. Recent advances in compact speech architectures now make it possible to run accurate, multilingual ASR and expressive TTS models entirely on device, all within a small memory footprint, with low latency, and completely offline. By pairing these lightweight speech models with on-device small language models (SLMs) like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4, developers can build apps that stream live transcriptions as the user speaks and synthesize natural spoken replies locally.

This release brings together major advancements across both Google’s system speech models and the open-weight ecosystem:

Below, we’ll explore how this local voice pipeline powers the new XR (Extended Reality) Language Teacher showcase, upgrades AI Chat in Google AI Edge Gallery, and how you can build with these APIs and models today.

Learning a language while traveling happens in the real world, often in underground stations, busy markets, or abroad where Wi-Fi and roaming data may not be available. To show how vision, language, and speech models can work together 100% locally on your phone with no network connection needed, we built the XR Language Teacher showcase in Google AI Edge Gallery. Simply point your camera at an unfamiliar object or menu, ask "What is that, and how do I ask for one?", and immediately hear an explanation in your language alongside authentic pronunciation in the local language.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

With inference running locally on a paired phone, the same experience works standalone on a phone and extends naturally to hands-free smart glasses. To see how we adapted Language Teacher for intelligent glasses check out our recent talk at Android Dev Summit: Building Audio-First Experiences for Intelligent Eyewear with Gemini Nano.

Beyond the XR Language Teacher showcase, we also built ASR and TTS directly into Google AI Edge Gallery’s main AI Chat feature, giving on-device SLMs like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4 a natural, offline voice interface while on the go.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

While multimodal models like Gemma 4 can process audio inputs directly, we provide a modular solution for cascading language models with separate, lightweight voice models for latency optimization and flexibility. Depending on your target devices and model requirements, our open-source code on GitHub shows you the optimized and recommended solution for Android using ML Kit, as well as MediaPipe Tasks and LiteRT for cross-platform coverage and open model support.

For developers building for Android devices, ML Kit’s GenAI Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech APIs provide a standardized, platform-integrated path out of the box. With the underlying speech models managed through AICore and Google’s on-device speech services, developers get production-grade quality without having to manage model downloads or worry about APK size bloat. Your app also benefits automatically from hardware acceleration across NPU and TPU backends (when available) and seamless background model updates.

Integrating ASR and TTS into cross-platform applications usually requires handling low-level audio framing, Voice Activity Detection (VAD), and complex decoding pipelines. MediaPipe Task simplifies this by providing unified, turnkey ASR and TTS APIs across iOS, macOS, Windows, Linux, and Web that abstract away manual pre-processing, audio stream routing, and platform-specific audio management into simple, high-level interfaces:

Under the hood, ML Kit and MediaPipe Tasks are powered by LiteRT, Google’s high-performance on-device AI inference infrastructure. Cross-platform applications requiring even more fine-grained control over speech processing or custom model architectures can also build on it directly.

Speech models often rely on multiple subgraphs and auxiliary assets, such as encoder/decoder subgraphs and tokenizers for ASR, or phonemizer dictionaries, voice embeddings, and vocoders for TTS. All required components for each model are packaged into a single .litertlm file for easy deployment. The app ships with ready-to-use .litertlm models, including Parakeet-TDT-0.6B-v3 (multilingual ASR), PocketTTS 100M (multilingual TTS), Phonon 31M (ultra-lightweight English TTS), and Kokoro-82M (multilingual TTS). Beyond these, the vibrant Hugging Face LiteRT Community with over 10K+ followers offers a growing catalog of open-weight models, like Whisper, Qwen3-ASR and Moonshine for ASR, or Qwen3-TTS and Matcha for TTS. For developers seeking custom implementations or support for additional open-weight voice models, LiteRT provides a rich set of multimodal model examples to help you get started, with more on the way.

Adding a local voice pipeline takes on-device SLMs beyond typing-based chat interfaces, enabling natural, voice-first interactions that work anywhere, completely offline. All the building blocks to try these experiences or integrate them into your own apps are available today:

We'd like to extend a special thanks to our significant contributors for their work on this project:

Anthropic takes on Google and Microsoft with Claude Sheets

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The company also has a new whiteboard app.

Anthropic is significantly expanding Claude's suite of productivity tools. At an event in San Francisco today, the company announced Claude Sheets. As you can probably guess, Sheets allows Claude to generate and edit spreadsheets for you. Each file can include its own set of tabs and formulas. You'll also find the usual assortment of formatting options.

Alongside Claude Sheets, Anthropic announced two other new products: Claude Meetings and Claude Whiteboards. The first of those is Anthropic's take on a meeting agent like the kind offered by Otter.ai and other companies. Available in Google Meet to start (which, incidentally, offers its own meeting agent through Gemini), Claude can join your video calls and start taking notes as you and your colleagues talk. You can later chat with Claude about any plans or action items that came up during your call. "Claude marks itself as a participant and posts a notice in the meeting's chat," says Anthropic. "It won't join a call with outside guests unless an admin allows it, and it leaves if one joins anyway." At the moment, Claude Meetings is only available to Team and Enterprise customers.

Moving to Claude Whiteboards, it's a pretty cutesy take on a collaborative brainstorming app. As your team works together, Claude will group everyone's ideas together by theme and draft action items for each individual. In a nifty bit of integration, you can use Claude Meetings and Whiteboards at the same time. Anthropic is billing the tool as a natural extension of how people already use Claude. "Many people already brainstorm with Claude, but sharing the results with coworkers has meant copy-pasting them somewhere else," the company says. Claude Whiteboards is available in beta to all paid users across the web, desktop and mobile.

Separately from those announcements, Anthropic is renaming Claude Dashboards to Claude Data and starting to roll out the tool to all paid users. Anthropic says it decided to rename the app because "companies use their data for more than dashboards." In any case, you can connect Claude Data to your company's CRM so that the chatbot can then answer specific questions you might have about what's inside.

Lastly, Anthropic is upgrading Claude's plugin functionality, which allows you to connect apps like Canva and Notion to the chatbot so it can pull information from those services. As Anthropic notes, there are already more than 9,000 connectors offered through the Claude Marketplace, but now you can open full apps inside of Claude, allowing the chatbot to act on what it sees in the window. Anthropic is rolling out this update starting with a small group of partners that includes Adobe, Atlassian and HubSpot.

Google Fi announces super network automatic carrier switching for improved speed and reception

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It’s rolling out first on Pixel phones before wider availability arrives in 2027.

Getting the fastest mobile data speeds can be tricky because a single carrier may not have optimal coverage in your area. So to address this, Google Fi is introducing its new super network feature which allows your phone to automatically switch between two different carriers to deliver the best connection possible.

The super network feature begins rolling out today to customers with Pixel phones (Pixel 9 and above with eSIMs). In the event that your phone detects that your connection is sluggish, it will automatically switch from one carrier to another. Unfortunately, Google Fi is not officially disclosing the names of the carriers it's using for its super network, though based on previous partnerships one of them is probably T-Mobile. Regardless, the effect is that if you hit a dead zone or congestion, your phone can connect to a different cell signal without any manual input.

However, that's not all, because alongside the super network, Google Fi is also introducing a new Wi-Fi Auto-Connect+ feature designed to improve data speeds indoors in places like malls, airports and more. That means even if you're in a building that interferes with traditional cellular reception, your phone can switch over to Wi-Fi where available, while automatic encryption ensures that your data stays secure. Wi-Fi Auto-Connect+ will initially be available on Pixel phones (Pixel 5a and newer) and some Motorola and Samsung phones (running OneUI 9 and above) across select locations throughout the US, Europe, Asia and Brazil.

But perhaps the best part is that both Google Fi's super network and Wi-Fi Auto Connect+ will be available to customers with Unlimited Premium and Flexible plans at no extra cost. Furthermore, for international travelers, both features will work in over 200 destinations across the globe.

Alternatively, for customers who need a little extra help or technical support, the company is rolling out its new AI-powered Ask Fi agent inside the app. It's designed to answer questions, straighten out billing concerns or help troubleshoot things like connectivity issues in real time. Thankfully, Google Fi says there will still be actual humans you can reach out to if that's what you prefer.

Personal Consumer Issues • Re: Sams Club vs Costco

While I (still) prefer Costco, Sam’s has made terrific customer-friendly changes in recent years and closed the gap considerably. Limited effective fleet supply growth due to an aging fleet, a growing share of vessels operating outside mainstream trades and a limited near-term newbuilding delivery schedule. 10 seat choices and habits that affect your health on a flight

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The healthiest seat on a plane depends on your goal, from fewer close contacts at the window to easier movement and circulation on the aisle

The seat that keeps you farthest from a coughing stranger is not the seat that does the most for your circulation. That tension sits at the center of any search for the healthiest seat on a plane. A window seat puts the cabin wall on one side and leaves a passenger with a neighbor on only one side. An aisle seat gives direct access to the aisle for standing, stretching and walking. Both choices protect something. Neither protects everything.

Seat selection affects more than comfort or price. It shapes how much contact a traveler has with other people, how often they get up and how well they sleep. Each position carries its own mix of exposure, movement, noise and light. The right choice depends on what a passenger is trying to protect on a given flight.

Some conditions apply to every seat in the cabin. Most large commercial jets mix cabin air with outside air and pass it through HEPA filters. Those filters remove at least 99.97 percent of particles of 0.3 microns. Cabin air generally flows in a mostly vertical pattern rather than along the length of the plane. That pattern limits how far airborne particles spread down the cabin.

Cabin pressure is shared, too. On most commercial flights, it is equivalent to an altitude of roughly 6,000 to 8,000 feet. No seat assignment changes that.

What does change from row to row is proximity. A passenger's neighbors, the people walking past and the activity around lavatories and galleys all depend on where a seat sits. So does access to the aisle, the amount of legroom and the motion felt during turbulence.

The 10 slides that follow cover a selection of seat positions and in-flight habits worth weighing before booking. They include the window, aisle and middle seats, rows near the front and over the wing, and seats beside lavatories, galleys, exits and bulkheads. They also cover seat choice for sleep and jet lag, along with habits that travel with a passenger to any row. Each one explains the trade-off involved. That lets travelers match a seat to their own priorities, whether the trip is a short hop or a long-haul journey.

Window-seat passengers had fewer contacts with potentially infectious people than passengers in middle or aisle seats in a 2018 model of transcontinental flights. For a traveler whose priority is limiting close contact, the window is the seat that model favors.

The research came from Emory University and Georgia Tech. Vicki Stover Hertzberg led the team. The work was published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences. The researchers modeled how infection could spread through the cabin on that type of flight.

Position in the row sets how many people sit within reach. A window passenger has one seatmate, with the side of the aircraft on the other. A middle passenger sits between two people. An aisle passenger shares an armrest with one neighbor and an open walkway with everyone else.

That walkway is where the gap widens. In the study, aisle passengers had more contact with people walking past them. Boarding passengers, travelers heading to the restroom and crew members all use the same narrow path. Each pass brings a stranger briefly close to the aisle seat. The window seat sits two places away from that traffic.

Window passengers also have fewer reasons to leave their seats. They have no direct access to the aisle. Every trip out means asking at least one neighbor to stand up or shift.

That habit carries a cost. Moving around during a long flight helps lower the risk of deep vein thrombosis, a blood clot that usually forms in the legs. A passenger who rarely leaves the window seat gets less of that movement. The window favors limiting contact, not circulation.

The study's scope has limits. It was a model of how infection could spread, not a count of confirmed infections among real passengers. It also covered only transcontinental flights. Its results do not automatically carry over to other routes, aircraft or flight lengths. A window seat reduced close contacts in that model. It does not make a passenger immune to an illness on board.

An aisle seat is the simplest way to get up and walk during a long flight without climbing past a neighbor. That access matters for circulation. The U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention warns that travel lasting four hours or more raises the risk of blood clots.

The main concern is deep vein thrombosis, or DVT. It is a clot that forms in a deep vein, most often in the leg. Sitting still for hours slows blood flow in the lower legs. That gives a clot a chance to form. The danger grows if part of the clot breaks loose and travels to the lungs. That event, called a pulmonary embolism, can be life-threatening.

For long trips, the CDC advises travelers to move around, stretch their legs and stay hydrated. An aisle seat removes the main obstacle to the first two. A passenger can stand, walk toward the back of the cabin and return without waking anyone or squeezing past a lowered tray table.

That ease can change behavior. Passengers in window and middle seats may stay put for hours rather than disturb the people beside them. Someone on the aisle can get up whenever the seatbelt sign is off, as often as needed.

The aisle also helps with leg exercises done while seated. The CDC suggests raising and lowering the heels with the toes on the floor, then raising the toes with the heels down. An aisle passenger can also extend a leg into the walkway when no cart or person is passing.

Risk is not equal for everyone. The CDC lists factors that raise clot risk, including older age, obesity, recent surgery or injury, pregnancy, estrogen-based birth control and a previous clot. Travelers $TRV in those groups gain the most from a seat that makes moving easy. The CDC also suggests they talk to a doctor about compression stockings before a long trip.

The trade-off is traffic. Aisle passengers sit beside everyone walking up and down the cabin, including crew with service carts and people heading to the lavatory. Elbows and shoulders on the aisle side get bumped more often. A traveler focused on circulation rather than distance from others may accept that cost in exchange for the freedom to move.

A middle seat puts a passenger within arm's reach of two people at once, one on each side. That position matters because exposure to an infectious traveler is concentrated in the seats closest to that person.

Researchers at Emory University and Georgia Tech studied this pattern in 2018. Their study, led by Vicki Stover Hertzberg and published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, modeled how infection could spread on transcontinental flights. It found that the highest risk of exposure fell within about one row and one seat of an infectious passenger. Most passengers seated farther away had little exposure.

A middle seat falls inside that zone for both neighbors in the same row. If either the window passenger or the aisle passenger is sick, the person in the middle sits directly beside them for the length of the flight.

The study modeled one type of flight. Its findings describe transcontinental routes, not every trip. The seating geometry holds on any flight. In a row of three seats, the middle seat has two direct neighbors, while the window and aisle seats each have one.

Movement is the second issue. A middle-seat passenger cannot reach the aisle without asking the aisle neighbor to stand or squeezing past them. That makes getting up during a long flight more awkward. Each trip out of the row also brings the passenger face to face with a seatmate in a tight space.

A traveler assigned a middle seat has a few practical options. Checking the airline's seat map after booking can show whether a window or aisle seat has opened up. Many airlines also allow seat changes at check-in, when the map can look different from the one shown at booking.

For passengers who stay in the middle, timing makes the row easier to manage. Standing up when the aisle neighbor is already out of their seat avoids asking them to stand again. A middle seat in a row with an empty neighbor seat also removes one of the two direct contacts.

A seat near the front of the cabin mainly shortens the time spent standing in a crowded aisle at the end of a flight. The benefit is practical rather than medical: fewer minutes packed close to other passengers, and a faster return to open space.

The gain is clearest after landing. Passengers usually leave the aircraft in rough row order, starting nearest the forward door. A traveler in one of the first rows can often reach the jet bridge while people farther back are still waiting to stand.

That wait is one of the most crowded stretches of any trip. People stand in the aisle, pull bags from overhead bins and lean over seated neighbors. Sitting near the front reduces how long a passenger spends in that queue.

Boarding works differently. Many airlines board premium cabins and status holders first, then call economy passengers in groups. Depending on the method, a forward economy seat can mean walking past fewer seated passengers to reach the row. It can also mean a later boarding group. By the time that group boards, the nearby overhead bins may already be full.

Boarding procedures differ by airline and sometimes by aircraft. A carrier's posted boarding order shows whether a forward seat actually means less time in the aisle on that flight.

A forward seat also helps on a tight connection. Getting off the aircraft sooner leaves more time to reach the next gate. That can mean walking through the terminal instead of hurrying with carry-on bags.

The location has limits. A front-row seat is not evidence of cleaner surfaces, better air or lower exposure to a sick passenger nearby. Its advantage is less time in a congested aisle, mostly after landing.

Some airlines sell forward economy rows as preferred seats for an extra fee. The cost varies by carrier, route and fare. Seat maps shown at booking usually mark which rows carry a surcharge.

Seats over the wing sit close to an aircraft's center of gravity, where turbulence is usually felt less than in the rear of the cabin. For travelers prone to motion sickness, a row above the wing can make the ride feel steadier.

The reason lies in how a plane moves. When rough air pushes on an aircraft, it tends to pitch nose-up and nose-down around its center of gravity. Rows near that pivot point travel a shorter vertical distance than rows near the tail. Passengers at the back feel the same jolt as a larger swing.

Aircraft are designed so that the center of gravity falls close to where the wings generate lift. That puts the steadiest part of the cabin roughly in line with the wings. No seat removes turbulence entirely, and a row over the wing still moves when the air is rough.

Motion sickness develops when the inner ear senses movement that the eyes do not see. Inside a cabin, seatbacks and overhead bins look still while the body registers each rise and drop. Smaller movements leave less of a gap between those two signals.

A window seat in an over-wing row adds another tool. Looking out at the horizon gives the eyes a fixed reference that lines up with what the inner ear feels. The wing blocks part of the view below, but the horizon usually stays in sight.

There are trade-offs. On many narrow-body jets, including the Boeing $BA 737 and Airbus A320 families, the engines hang beneath the wings. Rows beside them can pick up more engine noise than rows farther forward. Over-wing rows on these aircraft also often include emergency exits, which come with their own seating rules.

A steadier seat does not replace a fastened seatbelt. The Federal Aviation Administration advises passengers to keep their belts buckled whenever they are seated, even after the seatbelt sign goes off. Turbulence can arrive without warning, including in clear air with no visible clouds.

Rows next to an airplane lavatory see more people passing through and standing nearby than most other rows. They also take on more light and noise from the door and the toilet itself. Those conditions make a lavatory-side row a poor fit for passengers who want to rest or keep their distance from others.

On single-aisle jets such as the Boeing $BA 737 and Airbus A320 families, lavatories usually sit at the front and back of the cabin. Wide-body aircraft often add more lavatories in the middle sections. The rows directly beside those lavatories see the most bathroom traffic on any given flight.

Foot traffic near a lavatory is not just people walking by. Passengers often wait in the aisle for the door to open. They can stand beside the closest seats for minutes at a time. On a full flight, a short queue can form right next to someone trying to sleep or eat.

Each visit brings its own disruptions. The door opens, shuts and latches every time. Light from inside the lavatory spills into the cabin when the door opens during a darkened overnight flight. Most modern airliners use vacuum toilets. The flush is loud enough to carry into nearby rows.

The row directly in front of a rear lavatory has an added drawback. Seats backed against a wall or partition often recline only partly, or not at all. Airline seat maps show where each lavatory sits. That makes these rows easy to spot before booking.

The same location has a clear upside for some travelers. Passengers who are pregnant, managing a medical condition that requires frequent bathroom use or traveling with small children may value being a few steps away. Anyone drinking plenty of water on a long flight will also make more trips.

For those travelers, an aisle seat a few rows from the lavatory can be a middle path. It keeps the walk short without placing them in the line of every queue and door cycle.

Exit-row seats generally give passengers more legroom than standard economy rows, but they come with eligibility rules. Bulkhead seats also have no seat in front, though a fixed armrest can make them narrower.

That extra space matters most on a long flight. A passenger with room to straighten the knees and flex the ankles can change position often without standing up. Tall travelers, and anyone who stiffens in a cramped row, gain the most from that room.

The trade-off with an exit row is responsibility. Anyone seated there must meet requirements set by the airline and the aviation authority. That includes being able to help open the exit and assist others in an emergency. In the U.S., these rules come from the Federal Aviation Administration.

Under FAA rules, a passenger in an exit row must be at least 15 years old. The passenger must also be able to understand crew instructions and carry out the exit duties. Flight attendants confirm this before departure. A passenger who cannot or does not want to help can be reseated.

Exit rows also limit what can stay at a passenger's feet. Bags and loose items cannot block the path to the exit. Carry-ons usually go into the overhead bin for takeoff and landing. Some exit-row seats, and some seats directly ahead of an exit row, have limited or no recline.

Bulkhead seats sit directly behind a wall, curtain or partition rather than another row of passengers. With no seat in front, nobody reclines into the passenger's space. Getting out to the aisle can also be easier. There is no seatback to squeeze past.

The design brings its own compromises. The tray table usually folds out of the armrest, and that armrest is fixed in place. A fixed armrest can make the seat narrower than a standard one. That difference matters for wider travelers. There is also no under-seat storage ahead, so bags go overhead during takeoff and landing.

On many long-haul aircraft, bulkhead rows are where airlines fit bassinets for infants. That can mean more activity nearby on overnight flights.

Exact legroom and seat width vary by aircraft type and airline. The seat map for a specific flight shows where the exit rows and bulkheads sit before a traveler commits to one.

Rows beside an airplane galley sit next to the cabin's working kitchen. Those seats pick up more noise, more light and more crew movement than most others. That activity is the main trade-off of the location.

The galley is where flight attendants store service carts, heat meals, brew coffee and prepare drinks. On most single-aisle jets, galleys sit at the front and rear of the cabin. Many wide-body aircraft add a galley between cabin sections. That arrangement places some mid-cabin rows beside one too.

Noise arrives in bursts tied to the service schedule. Carts roll out and lock back into their bays. Drawers and storage compartments open and shut. Crew members talk through each round of service, and the sound carries from setup through cleanup.

Light is the second factor. A galley usually stays lit while the crew works, even after the main cabin lights are dimmed for an overnight flight. Some aircraft have a curtain between the galley and the nearest seats. A curtain softens the glare but does not block sound.

Crew traffic adds a third layer. Meal and drink carts typically begin their run at a galley. That places nearby rows at the start or end of each service pass. Flight attendants also walk past these rows often to restock supplies and collect trash. On long-haul flights, passengers sometimes stand near the galley to stretch. That brings more people close to the adjacent seats.

Food smells come with the location as well. Meals are heated in galley ovens, and the aroma reaches the closest rows first. Travelers $TRV prone to nausea may want to weigh that alongside the noise.

How much these trade-offs matter depends on the trip. On a short daytime flight, galley activity is mostly background. On an overnight route, it can interrupt the hours a traveler planned to sleep.

Airline seat maps and booking sites usually mark where galleys sit on a given aircraft. Checking the map before choosing a seat shows which rows border the galley. Moving even a few rows away puts more distance between a passenger and the busiest stretch of the cabin.

For sleep on a long flight, a window seat offers two practical advantages. It provides a fixed cabin wall to lean against, and it gives the passenger control of the window shade, which governs how much daylight reaches the eyes.

The side panel supports the head and shoulder in a way an aisle or middle seat cannot. Passengers in those seats either sit upright or end up leaning toward a stranger. A travel pillow also holds its shape better when it is braced against a solid surface.

A window seat removes one common cause of broken sleep. No neighbor needs to climb past to reach the aisle. A sleeping passenger is therefore less likely to be woken partway through the flight. The trade-off runs the other way, too. A window passenger who wants to get up has to wake someone else.

The shade matters for more than comfort. Light is the main signal that sets the body's internal clock, known as the circadian rhythm. Bright light suppresses melatonin, the hormone that helps bring on sleep. Darkness allows melatonin levels to rise.

That makes timing useful for jet lag. Light in the morning tends to shift the body clock earlier. Light in the evening tends to push it later. A traveler flying east generally needs to move their clock earlier. One flying west needs to move it later.

Opening or closing the shade to match the light schedule a destination calls for can begin that adjustment before landing. Eastbound trips are usually harder on the body than westbound ones. The human body clock tends to run slightly longer than 24 hours, which makes lengthening the day easier than shortening it.

Shade control has limits. Cabin crew on some flights ask passengers to lower shades so the cabin stays dark for sleep. The Boeing $BA 787 replaces pull-down shades with electronically dimmable windows that crew can adjust centrally.

Screens add a second light source close to the face. Seatback monitors and phones give off light that can delay sleepiness. Dimming them or switching them off helps when the goal is rest.

Recline deserves a check before booking. Seats in the last row of a cabin often sit against a wall or partition and may recline little or not at all. Reviewing the seat map for the specific aircraft can help a traveler avoid those rows.

What a passenger does during a flight affects health risk alongside where they sit. Clean hands, steady water intake and regular movement help in every seat in the cabin, from the window to the aisle.

Many travelers handle the surfaces within reach of any seat over a day of flying. Tray tables, seatbelt buckles, overhead air vents and seatback touchscreens are among the most frequently touched. The seatback pocket and armrests see heavy use too. A passenger has no way of knowing how recently any of them was cleaned.

Hand hygiene links all of these surfaces. Washing with soap and water reduces the chance that something picked up from a shared surface reaches the eyes, nose or mouth. An alcohol-based hand sanitizer is a practical substitute when a sink is out of reach. The moments that matter most are before eating, after using the lavatory and after handling shared items.

Disinfecting wipes add a second layer. Wiping the tray table, armrests, buckle and screen after sitting down takes a minute or two. That covers the surfaces a passenger will touch most for the rest of the flight. Keeping hands away from the face between cleanings serves the same purpose.

Hydration is the second habit. The U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention lists staying hydrated among its recommendations for long trips. Water and other nonalcoholic drinks serve that goal better than alcohol. Alcohol increases the amount of fluid the body loses.

Planning ahead makes this easier. An empty reusable bottle can pass through airport security. It can then be filled near the gate before boarding. That removes dependence on the timing of the cabin drink service.

Movement is the third habit. When the seatbelt sign is off, a short walk to the back of the cabin and back gets the legs working. Passengers who must stay seated, such as during turbulence or a meal service, still have options. They can flex their ankles, press their feet into the floor and roll their shoulders.

None of these habits depends on a seat assignment. A window passenger and an aisle passenger can follow the same routine. The seat sets the starting conditions, and the habits apply in every one of them.
